Part V: Plant Selection and Guilds (Chapters 26–32)

Chapter 26: Companion Planting (Mechanism, Not Folklore)

The traditional literature on companion planting is rife with folklore and anthropomorphism—the idea that "tomatoes love basil" or "marigolds are the gardener's best friend." Some pairings can work, but the mechanism and evidence tier matter. A regenerative methodology should distinguish allelopathy, push-pull dynamics, trap cropping, insectary value, resource partitioning, and folklore rather than treating all companion claims as equal.

Allelopathy: Biochemical Interactions in the Soil

Allelopathy is the biological phenomenon by which an organism produces biochemicals that influence the germination, growth, survival, and reproduction of other organisms (Rice, 1984; Inderjit & Duke, 2003). These allelochemicals can be beneficial or detrimental.

Figure LSE_B24

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Allelopathy: Biochemical Interactions in the Soil

  • Negative Allelopathy: The classic example is the Black Walnut tree (Juglans nigra), which exudes juglone, a compound highly toxic to many solanaceous crops (Willis, 2000). Similarly, cereal rye exudes allelochemicals that inhibit the germination of small-seeded weeds, making it an excellent cover crop but a poor companion for direct-seeded carrots.
  • Positive or Suppressive Interactions: Certain plants may exude compounds that influence neighboring plants or microbes. For example, specific Allium species have shown suppressive effects against some soil-borne fungi in some studies, but this should be presented as an evidence-tiered companion strategy, not as a Fusarium control program or replacement for resistant varieties, sanitation, drainage, and rotation.

The Push-Pull Strategy

The push-pull strategy is an evidence-supported pest-management approach in specific crop-pest systems that uses companion plants to manipulate insect behavior (Khan et al., 1997; Cook et al., 2007).

Figure LSE_E12

Placement: inline after 2nd para: The Push-Pull Strategy

  • The Push (Repellent): Plants that emit volatile organic compounds (VOCs) that may repel or confuse target pests are intercropped with the primary crop. For example, in the classic ICIPE research, Desmodium is planted between rows of maize to repel stemborer moths. (Note: The common garden advice that thyme repels cabbage moths is a folkloric extrapolation of this concept; the masking mechanism evidence is weaker than the trap-crop evidence ).
  • The Pull (Trap Crop): Plants that are highly attractive to the target pest are planted on the perimeter of the field to "pull" the insects away from the cash crop. In the ICIPE model, Napier grass is planted on the borders to attract the stemborer moths away from the maize.

Resource Partitioning

Evidence-tiered companion planting can improve the use of space, light, and nutrients by pairing plants with complementary architectures and life cycles. The classic "Three Sisters" guild (corn, beans, squash) is a well-known example of resource partitioning (Mt. Pleasant, 2006): corn provides a vertical trellis, beans can fix nitrogen when compatible Rhizobia are present, and squash can shade the soil as a living mulch. The nitrogen contribution and weed suppression depend on timing, variety, density, soil fertility, and water availability.

North Texas Note: The "Three Sisters" guild can struggle in North Texas because extreme summer heat and irrigation needs can put corn, beans, and squash on different stress schedules, and a dense squash understory can increase humidity. A more locally adapted guild to trial is pairing okra with a sprawling, heat-tolerant ground cover like sweet potatoes, while still monitoring water demand, airflow, and pest pressure.

Chapter 27: Polyculture, Edge Effect, and Guild Design

Monoculture—the cultivation of a single crop over a large area—is uncommon in natural ecosystems and often requires external management to maintain. Polyculture, the simultaneous cultivation of multiple crops in the same space, is one regenerative design strategy for increasing functional diversity and habitat complexity.

Figure UC-015 ◆

Placement: inline near target heading: Chapter 27: Polyculture, Edge Effect, and Guild Design

Figure UC-016 ◆

Placement: inline near target heading: Chapter 27: Polyculture, Edge Effect, and Guild Design

The Edge Effect

In ecology, the "edge effect" refers to changes in species composition, movement, microclimate, and interaction at the boundary between two habitats (Odum, 1971; Forman, 1995). Edges can be more diverse or productive in some systems, but they can also increase exposure, pest movement, predation, drying, or weed pressure. Polyculture design can use height, root depth, bloom timing, and canopy structure to create useful habitat complexity, but edge effects are variable rather than automatically beneficial.

Guild Design: The Seven-Layer System

A "guild" is a permaculture design framework—a grouping of plants intended to stack functions such as shade, mulch, nitrogen fixation, pollinator habitat, groundcover, and harvest. The seven-layer model is a useful practitioner design tool, not a universal ecological law. A common permaculture guild consists of seven layers:

Figure LSE_B25

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Guild Design: The Seven-Layer System

  1. Canopy: Large fruit or nut trees (e.g., Pecan).
  2. Sub-Canopy: Dwarf fruit trees (e.g., Peach or Plum).
  3. Shrub: Berry bushes (e.g., Blackberry).
  4. Herbaceous: Non-woody plants that die back in winter (e.g., Comfrey, Echinacea).
  5. Groundcover: Sprawling plants that protect the soil (e.g., Sweet Potato, Creeping Thyme).
  6. Root/Rhizosphere: Plants that yield below ground (e.g., Garlic, Daikon Radish).
  7. Vertical/Vine: Climbing plants that utilize the canopy for support (e.g., Passionflower, Malabar Spinach).

A Worked Example: The Texas Peach Tree Guild

Instead of planting a peach tree in an isolated circle of mulch, design a guild around it:

  • Center: The Peach Tree (Sub-Canopy).
  • Nitrogen Fixer: A shrubby legume like False Indigo (Amorpha fruticosa) planted nearby to add a nitrogen-fixing function where compatible rhizobia, moisture, and establishment conditions are present.
  • Chop-and-Drop Biomass: Comfrey planted at the drip line for mulch biomass; treat dynamic-accumulator mineral claims as site-dependent unless supported by tissue or soil analysis.
  • Pest-Confusion / Companion Layer: Garlic or chives planted near the trunk may contribute scent diversity or allium-family companion value, but should not be presented as stand-alone borer control.
  • Insectary: Yarrow and Echinacea planted on the sunny side can provide floral resources for some beneficial insects when bloom timing and habitat conditions fit.

North Texas Note: Polyculture can be useful for managing the intense solar radiation of the Texas summer. Planting tall, heat-tolerant crops like sunflowers or amaranth on the western edge of a bed can cast afternoon shade over more sensitive crops, but spacing, airflow, water competition, and pest habitat still need to be managed.

Chapter 28: The Insectary and Pollinator Guilds

A living soil system does not function in isolation; it is connected to above-ground ecology. One important component of this integration is the establishment of insectary and pollinator guilds—dedicated plantings designed to attract, feed, and shelter beneficial insects.

The Role of the Insectary

An insectary is not merely a flower garden; it is habitat intended to support biological control agents. The goal is to attract predatory and parasitic insects, such as ladybugs, lacewings, syrphid flies, and parasitic wasps, that may prey on or parasitize some crop pests when pest species, habitat, timing, and pesticide practices allow.

Figure LSE_A14

Placement: inline after 2nd para: The Role of the Insectary

Figure LSE_E07

Placement: inline after 2nd para: The Role of the Insectary

Floral Architecture and Nectar Accessibility

Beneficial insects have different anatomical requirements. Many small parasitoids, including Trichogramma wasps, have short mouthparts and may use shallow, exposed nectar sources more readily than deep, tubular flowers. Therefore, an effective insectary often includes plants with shallow, accessible nectaries.

Figure LSE_B26

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Floral Architecture and Nectar Accessibility

  • Umbellifers: Plants in the carrot family (Apiaceae), such as dill, fennel, cilantro, and Queen Anne's lace, produce large, flat umbels composed of many small, accessible flowers. These are often useful attractants for parasitic wasps and syrphid flies.
  • Composites: Plants in the aster family (Asteraceae), such as yarrow, chamomile, and sunflowers, provide landing surfaces and pollen resources for larger visitors such as ladybugs and soldier beetles.

Figure LSE_E10

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Floral Architecture and Nectar Accessibility

Continuous Bloom and Habitat

To maintain beneficial-insect activity, the insectary should provide as continuous a succession of blooms as practical from early spring through late fall. If nectar, pollen, prey, or shelter are absent for long periods, beneficial populations may decline or move elsewhere. Dense, undisturbed vegetation can also provide shelter from extreme weather and overwintering habitat.

Figure UC-013

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Continuous Bloom and Habitat

Methodology Comparison: Pest Management and Insectaries

  • Soil Food Web (Ingham): As a practitioner framework, often interprets pest pressure as a sign of plant or soil imbalance. Improved soil biology may improve plant resilience, but it does not replace pest identification, monitoring, thresholds, sanitation, exclusion, resistant varieties, and habitat design.
  • Coot's Mix (Bennett): As a practitioner/community protocol, uses neem/karanja, kelp, compost, and mulch to support plant vigor and soil biology. Systemic unpalatability to pests should not be presented as guaranteed.
  • Bionutrient (Kittredge): As a Bionutrient practitioner framework, links plant nutrition, sap chemistry, and pest susceptibility. The claim that insects cannot digest complex proteins should not be stated as an entomological law; high plant health may reduce susceptibility to some pests under some conditions.
  • KNF/JADAM (Cho): As practitioner protocols, may use JHS or related inputs as botanical pest interventions, but these should be applied only after pest identification and threshold assessment, with toxic-plant, caustic-ingredient, pollinator, and beneficial-insect cautions.
  • Biodynamics (Steiner): As biodynamic practitioner doctrine, includes practices such as peppering. Present this as doctrine rather than established pest-deterrence mechanism, and do not use it as a substitute for IPM.

Figure LSE_E11

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Continuous Bloom and Habitat

Safety Box: Organic Pesticides, JHS, and Beneficial Insects Organic-compatible or homemade pest interventions are not automatically harmless. Use pest identification, monitoring, and action thresholds before spraying. Botanical preparations, including JADAM Herb Solution, can affect pollinators, predators, parasitoids, and people; do not casually use toxic plants such as oleander or nightshade. If a JADAM wetting agent or other caustic soap-making input is involved, potassium hydroxide/KOH requires gloves, eye protection, long sleeves, ventilation, clear labels, and secure storage. Avoid spraying open flowers or active pollinator periods unless the product/protocol specifically allows it.

North Texas Note: The brutal heat of August often causes traditional insectary plants like cilantro and dill to bolt or decline, creating a gap in nectar availability just as pest pressures may rise. To bridge this gap, incorporate native or adapted heat-tolerant plants such as Gregg's Mistflower (Conoclinium greggii) and Autumn Sage (Salvia greggii), while confirming bloom timing and invasiveness for the specific site.

Chapter 29: Crop Rotation and Succession Planning

Crop rotation—the practice of moving plant families to different locations over time—is a foundational tenet of organic agriculture. In a highly diverse, biologically active no-till system, rotation may be more flexible, but it remains important for managing host-specific pathogens, nematodes, nutrient demand, and residue patterns while also supporting succession planning.

The No-Till Paradigm Shift on Rotation

In a conventional system, planting tomatoes in the same spot repeatedly can increase host-specific pathogen pressure, including early blight, when the host, pathogen, and environment align. Biologically active soils may contribute to disease suppression through competition, antagonism, predation, and improved plant vigor, but disease-suppressive soils are context-dependent and do not guarantee that no pathogen can dominate (Mazzola, 2004; Weller et al., 2002). Rotation, sanitation, resistant varieties, airflow, drainage, residue management, and monitoring still matter in living soil systems.

Figure LSE_A15

Placement: inline after 2nd para: The No-Till Paradigm Shift on Rotation

Strategic Rotation for Soil Building

While rotation may be less rigid in some mature living-soil systems, it remains a powerful tool for disease management and soil building.

  • The Heavy Feeder to Light Feeder Shift: A heavy-feeding crop such as corn can be followed by a lighter-feeding crop such as carrots when soil structure, nutrient status, and season support the sequence.
  • The Legume Integration: Where practical, following or interplanting heavy feeders with a compatible legume can help rebuild nitrogen through rhizobial symbiosis, especially when the appropriate inoculant or native rhizobia are present.

Succession Planning: The Continuous Harvest

Succession planting is the art of timing. It helps reduce empty-bed periods and keeps living roots in the soil more consistently.

Figure LSE_B28

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Succession Planning: The Continuous Harvest

  • Sequential Sowing: Instead of planting a 20-foot row of lettuce all at once, plant smaller sections at intervals. This supports a more continuous, manageable harvest when weather and germination cooperate.
  • Succession by Season: As a spring crop like radishes nears harvest, interplant the summer crop like peppers directly into the same bed when spacing, disease risk, and transplant timing allow. This can maintain living roots and canopy cover without relying on edge-effect claims as a guarantee.

Figure LSE_C08

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Succession Planning: The Continuous Harvest

North Texas Note: Living soil may help suppress some diseases, but the Southern Root-Knot Nematode (Meloidogyne incognita) can remain aggressive in sandy loam soils. Even in a healthy system, rotate highly susceptible crops like tomatoes and okra, monitor roots at crop removal, and use resistant varieties, sanitation, organic-matter management, and nematode-suppressive cover crops where appropriate.

Chapter 30: Heat-Tough Crops and Cool-Season Production

The defining characteristic of North Texas agriculture is its seasonal contrast: brutal heat in July and August, followed by a surprisingly productive cool season from roughly November through March. Maximizing yield requires selecting crops adapted to these extremes.

Surviving the Texas Summer

When temperatures exceed 95°F, many common vegetables such as tomatoes, peppers, and squash experience physiological stress. Pollen viability, flower retention, and fruit set can decline, contributing to blossom drop. To maintain production, the grower can pivot toward crops better adapted to tropical, subtropical, or arid conditions.

Figure LSE_D13

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Surviving the Texas Summer

  • Okra (Abelmoschus esculentus): Thrives in extreme heat and drought. Its deep taproot accesses subsoil moisture.
  • Sweet Potatoes (Ipomoea batatas): A tropical vine that creates a dense living mulch, shades the soil, and can produce strong tuber yields when the season, soil, and water are appropriate.
  • Southern Peas/Cowpeas (Vigna unguiculata): Black-eyed peas and yardlong beans are heat-tolerant legumes that can fix nitrogen when compatible rhizobia are present.
  • Armenian Cucumber (Cucumis melo var. flexuosus): A heat-loving melon that produces crisp, cucumber-like fruit long after traditional cucumbers have succumbed to heat.

Figure LSE_D14

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Surviving the Texas Summer

Figure LSE_D17

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Surviving the Texas Summer

The Hidden Bounty: Cool-Season Production

Many Texas gardeners abandon their plots in October, missing one of the more forgiving growing windows of the year. From November to March, pest pressure is often lower, and the mild temperatures allow for the cultivation of cool-season crops.

  • Brassicas: Kale, collards, cabbage, and broccoli often thrive in cool weather. A light frost can improve perceived sweetness in some crops.
  • Alliums: Garlic and short-day onions are typically planted in October/November to overwinter and bulb up in the spring.
  • Root Crops: Carrots, beets, and turnips can grow slowly through the winter and often hold well in the soil when drainage, freeze protection, and pest pressure are managed.
  • Hardy Greens: Spinach, mache, and claytonia can tolerate freezes, with protection needs depending on cultivar, wind, soil moisture, and freeze duration.

Managing the Easter Freeze

The primary risk of cool-season growing in Texas is the unpredictable late-season freeze. Growers should be prepared to deploy frost cloth or low tunnels over sensitive crops when local forecasts threaten the crop's cold tolerance; the degree of protection depends on material, setup, wind, soil moisture, and exposure.

Figure LSE_E03

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Managing the Easter Freeze

North Texas Note: Do not fight the Texas summer; lean into it. By mid-July, consider pulling exhausted spring tomatoes and planting a cowpea cover crop, or transition the bed to okra and sweet potatoes. In many gardens, this produces better results with less water and stress than trying to keep spring crops alive through peak heat.

Chapter 31: Integrating Culinary and Medicinal Herbs

Herbs can be ecological anchors of the regenerative garden. Unlike annual vegetables, many herbs establish longer-lived root systems and relationships with soil biology, while also providing culinary value and, in some traditions, herbal uses for the grower.

The Ecological Function of Herbs

Aromatic herbs can serve several ecological functions in the living soil system:

Figure LSE_B29

Placement: inline after 2nd para: The Ecological Function of Herbs

  • Pest Deterrence / Scent Diversity: Their volatile organic compounds (VOCs) may confuse or repel some pests in specific crop-pest settings, but VOC masking should be treated as an evidence-tiered companion-planting claim, not a universal pest-control mechanism.
  • Fungal Support: Many woody herbs can form associations with arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF), but host compatibility and fungal persistence are species- and site-specific.

The Medicinal Garden Block

A dedicated herb block can provide culinary plants, insectary habitat, and plants used in traditional herbal practice. These plants are often resilient and require modest fertility, but human medicinal use should follow appropriate herbal or medical guidance.

Figure LSE_C17

Placement: inline after 2nd para: The Medicinal Garden Block

  • Comfrey (Symphytum officinale): A common chop-and-drop biomass plant in practitioner systems; dynamic-accumulator claims should be treated as site-dependent unless supported by tissue analysis. Comfrey also has traditional topical uses, but human medical use is outside this soil-management scope.
  • Calendula (Calendula officinalis): A prolific bloomer whose resinous flowers are traditionally harvested for skin oils and salves.
  • Echinacea (Echinacea purpurea): A native prairie plant used in traditional herbalism; avoid presenting it as a guaranteed immune intervention.
  • Holy Basil / Tulsi (Ocimum tenuiflorum): A highly aromatic herb that can thrive in Texas heat and attract native bees when in bloom.
  • Chamomile, Valerian, and Yarrow: These plants are also used in biodynamic practitioner preparations (Preps 502, 503, 507).

Figure LSE_C19

Placement: inline after 2nd para: The Medicinal Garden Block

Figure LSE_D20

Placement: inline after 2nd para: The Medicinal Garden Block

Strategic Placement of Culinary Herbs

Culinary herbs can be integrated into vegetable beds to add floral resources, scent diversity, groundcover, and harvest value. Woody perennial herbs like rosemary and lavender can be placed on garden perimeters as habitat and possible scent-diversity elements, but they should not be treated as a permanent pest barrier. Low-growing herbs such as creeping thyme or oregano can serve as living mulch where water, light, and competition are managed.

Figure LSE_C16

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Strategic Placement of Culinary Herbs

North Texas Note: Many Mediterranean herbs (rosemary, thyme, oregano) can thrive in the heat and alkaline soil of North Texas, but they are prone to decline in poorly drained, waterlogged conditions. If planting them in heavy clay, improve drainage with appropriate mineral aggregate, raised planting, or containers, especially during wet spring months.

Figure LSE_C18

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Strategic Placement of Culinary Herbs

Figure LSE_E05

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Strategic Placement of Culinary Herbs

Chapter 32: Cut Flowers as Biological Cover and Habitat

The integration of cut flowers into a vegetable production system is often viewed as a purely aesthetic choice. In a regenerative model, cut flowers can also serve as insectary habitat, seasonal biological cover, and a source of above-ground diversity.

Flowers as Biological Cover

When grown densely, cut flowers can function as a living mulch. A thick stand of zinnias or cosmos may shade the soil surface, suppress some weed germination, and reduce evaporation when stand density and irrigation are appropriate. Their root architectures and exudates can also add diversity to the rhizosphere, though microbiome effects vary by species, soil, and season.

Cut Flowers as Insectary Habitat

Cut flowers can be a strong expression of the insectary guild. By selecting a diverse mix of species with varying bloom times and flower architectures, the grower can provide a more continuous food source for predatory insects and pollinators, but this does not guarantee resident beneficial populations.

  • Sunflowers (Helianthus annuus): Can act as trap plants for some pests, including leaf-footed bugs or aphids in some gardens, while providing pollen for native bees. Monitor them so they do not become unmanaged pest reservoirs.
  • Zinnias (Zinnia elegans): Provide long-blooming nectar resources for butterflies and some beneficial insects when kept healthy and flowering.
  • Celosia (Celosia argentea): The dense flower heads may provide habitat structure for some small predators such as minute pirate bugs and predatory mites.
  • Cosmos (Cosmos bipinnatus): Their open, daisy-like structure provides accessible landing surfaces for syrphid flies, ladybugs, and other visitors.

North Texas Note: To survive the Texas summer, focus on heat-loving, drought-tolerant flower species. Zinnias, gomphrena, celosia, and native sunflowers can bloom through hot weather when established and irrigated appropriately, whereas delicate spring flowers like sweet peas or snapdragons often decline quickly in peak heat.

Placement: Chapter 39: IPM Foundations (Integrated Pest Management)

Integrated Pest Management (IPM) is a systematic, ecological approach to managing pests. In a regenerative system, the goal is never eradication; the goal is to maintain pest populations below the threshold of economic or aesthetic damage by leveraging the ecosystem's natural checks and balances.

The IPM Hierarchy

IPM operates on a strict hierarchy of interventions, moving from the least disruptive to the most disruptive.

  1. Cultural Control: The foundation. This includes selecting disease-resistant varieties, proper spacing for airflow, crop rotation, sanitation, and optimizing soil and water management to support plant tolerance and resilience.
  2. Mechanical/Physical Control: Physical barriers and removal. This includes using floating row covers (Agribon) to exclude cabbage moths, hand-picking hornworms, pruning infested tissue, or using water pressure to dislodge aphids from foliage.
  3. Biological Control: Leveraging predators and parasitoids. This involves planting insectaries to attract parasitic wasps and ladybugs, conserving native predators, or releasing purchased biological agents (like predatory mites or nematodes) when conditions match their requirements.
  4. Chemical Control (The Last Resort): The use of pesticides. In a living soil system, this is restricted to targeted, label-following products, including organic-approved compounds such as Bt for susceptible caterpillars or Spinosad where appropriate. These are deployed only after identification, monitoring, and threshold/action criteria justify intervention.

Scouting and Thresholds

IPM requires active observation. The grower must "scout" the garden regularly, turning over leaves and identifying the specific pest and its life stage.

Figure LSE_C03

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Scouting and Thresholds

  • Action Thresholds: Seeing three aphids on a tomato plant does not warrant a chemical intervention; it warrants observation and follow-up scouting. A growing aphid colony that is curling and stunting new growth may cross an action threshold, triggering a mechanical response such as a water blast, a biological response such as conserving or releasing lacewings, or a label-guided pesticide only if lower-disruption tactics are insufficient.

Methodology Comparison: Pest Management Approaches These are practitioner frameworks or doctrines unless independently supported for a specific pest, crop, product, and setting.

  • Soil Food Web (Ingham): Soil Food Web practitioners view pests as one possible symptom of biological or plant-health imbalance. Improved soil biology may contribute to plant resilience, but it does not make pests impossible or replace scouting and IPM thresholds.
  • Coot's Mix (Bennett): Coot's Mix practitioner protocols often use neem, karanja, kelp, and crustacean meals as part of a pest-resilience strategy; effects are ingredient-, rate-, pest-, soil-, and crop-dependent, not guaranteed systemic pest control.
  • Bionutrient (Kittredge): The claim that insects cannot digest complex proteins belongs to the Bionutrient/Kempf practitioner framework. It should be treated as a plant-health heuristic about reduced free nitrates/simple amino acids and possible lower susceptibility, not as a universal entomological law.
  • KNF/JADAM (Cho): KNF/JADAM practitioner protocols may use JHS (JADAM Herb Solution) and JWA as direct botanical pesticide tools when thresholds are crossed. Because these can affect beneficials and may involve toxic plants or caustic ingredients, they require safety controls and careful timing.
  • Biodynamics (Steiner): Biodynamic pest "peppering" is practitioner/organizational doctrine. Mechanism not established in peer-reviewed literature; the methodology is surveyed at this level only per Book 1 scope.

Safety Box: Organic Pesticides and Biological Controls Organic-approved does not mean harmless. Bt, Spinosad, soaps, oils, JHS/JWA mixtures, and biological insecticides must be matched to the pest and life stage, used according to the product label or tested practitioner protocol, and timed to reduce harm to pollinators and beneficial insects. Avoid spraying open flowers or active pollinator periods unless the label explicitly allows it. Wear appropriate protection, keep mixtures labeled, and store them away from children, pets, and food.

North Texas Note: The Squash Vine Borer is a serious pest in North Texas because larvae feed inside stems, making late intervention difficult. Treat Bt stem injection or directed Bt application as one tactic among several, not the only effective strategy. Cultural and mechanical options include planting less-susceptible Cucurbita moschata varieties such as Tromboncino, using row cover before flowering, scouting for eggs, removing infested vines, timing plantings, and using trap or succession plantings where appropriate.

Chapter 40: Beneficial Predators and Habitat

A healthy garden supports a complex above-ground food web. The regenerative grower encourages resident predatory and parasitic insects to help keep pest populations below damaging thresholds. Understanding the life cycles and habitat requirements of these beneficials is critical for biological control.

The Major Predators

  • Ladybugs (Coccinellidae): Both the adults and the alligator-like larvae consume aphids. However, purchased ladybugs often disperse quickly. Attracting and conserving native populations through habitat plantings is usually more durable.
  • Green Lacewings (Chrysopidae): The adults feed on nectar, but the larvae (known as "aphid lions") are important predators of aphids, thrips, and spider mites.
  • Syrphid Flies (Hoverflies): Adults mimic the appearance of bees or wasps but do not sting. They are valuable pollinators, and their slug-like larvae consume aphids.
  • Parasitic Wasps (e.g., Trichogramma, Braconid): These tiny, non-stinging wasps lay their eggs inside the bodies of pests (like the Tomato Hornworm or various aphids). The hatching larvae consume the pest from the inside out, eventually spinning visible white cocoons on the host's body.
  • Predatory Mites (e.g., Phytoseiulus persimilis): Microscopic hunters that actively seek out and consume spider mites, a major pest in hot, dry climates.
  • Assassin Bugs and Ground Beetles: Generalist predators that hunt caterpillars, grubs, and other soft-bodied insects.
  • Spiders: Important generalist predators that provide passive trapping and hunting of many flying or crawling insects.
  • Insectivorous Birds: Chickadees, warblers, and wrens can consume many caterpillars and aphids during the breeding season.

Figure LSE_B33

Placement: inline after 2nd para: The Major Predators

Providing Habitat and Overwintering Sites

Beneficial insects require more than just nectar; they require undisturbed habitat to shelter from extreme weather and to overwinter.

  • Beetle Banks: A raised berm planted with native bunch grasses provides a dry, insulated environment for ground beetles and spiders to overwinter.
  • Leaving the Stems: Do not cut down perennial herbaceous plants (like Echinacea or native sunflowers) in the fall. The hollow stems provide critical overwintering habitat for native solitary bees and parasitic wasps.
  • Overwintering Mulch: A thick layer of undisturbed leaf litter or straw can provide insulation for overwintering ladybugs and lacewings.
  • Bare Ground: Leave small patches of un-mulched, well-drained soil to provide nesting habitat for ground-nesting native bees and predatory wasps.
  • Water Sources: In the extreme heat of summer, beneficial insects need water. A shallow dish filled with pebbles and water provides a safe drinking station without the risk of drowning.

North Texas Note: The heat of August often causes traditional insectary plants (like cilantro and dill) to bolt and decline, creating a gap in nectar availability just as pest pressure can rise. To bridge this gap, incorporate native, heat-loving Texas perennials like Greg's Mistflower (Conoclinium greggii) and Autumn Sage (Salvia greggii), which can bloom through hot weather. Providing a reliable, shallow water source is also important for beneficial insects when temperatures exceed 100°F for extended periods.

Chapter 41: Foliar Applications and Plant Sap Health

Foliar feeding—the application of liquid nutrients or biological products to the leaves—can be a useful tool for short-term, targeted support when the material, crop stage, weather, and label or protocol are appropriate. In this manuscript, John Kempf's Plant Health Pyramid should be treated as a Bionutrient practitioner framework, not as a peer-reviewed universal law.

The Mechanism of Foliar Absorption

Plants may absorb some foliar-applied materials through the cuticle, stomatal pathways, or other leaf-surface routes, but uptake varies by species, leaf age, formulation, droplet drying time, humidity, temperature, and nutrient form. Early morning or evening applications often reduce heat and evaporation stress, but stomatal opening alone does not guarantee uptake.

Targeted Applications Within the Plant Health Pyramid Framework

In the Kempf/Bionutrient practitioner strategy, foliar sprays are used to support plant nutrition and sap chemistry when soil uptake is limited or when sap/tissue testing identifies a specific need.

  • Tier 1 & 2 (Complete Photosynthesis & Protein Synthesis): If testing or repeated field observation suggests a nitrogen, magnesium, sulfur, molybdenum, or trace-mineral constraint, a label-guided foliar such as fish hydrolysate, kelp extract, or a specific mineral product may provide short-term support. Do not frame this as making plants invisible to aphids or rapidly correcting every deficiency.
  • Tier 3 & 4 (Lipid Synthesis & Secondary Metabolites): Bionutrient practitioners may use specific micronutrients or biological ferments such as FFJ or FPJ to support later-stage physiology. This should be presented as a practitioner strategy whose effects depend on crop, diagnosis, material quality, dilution, timing, and weather—not as consensus proof that foliar sprays move plants through fixed immunity tiers.

The Role of Surfactants

Water has high surface tension and tends to bead up and roll off waxy leaves. A surfactant (wetting agent) can improve coverage, but it can also increase phytotoxicity or move materials into tissues faster than intended.

  • Natural Surfactants: Yucca extract, aloe vera extract, or JADAM Wetting Agent (JWA) may be used when appropriate, but they should not be included automatically in every foliar application. Follow product labels or tested practitioner protocols, and test a small area before broad use.

Safety Box: Foliar Sprays, JWA, Ferments, and Compost Tea Apply foliar products according to label or tested protocol, avoid heat and intense sun, and test a small area before treating the whole crop. Homemade JWA is made with potassium hydroxide (KOH), which is caustic; use gloves, eye protection, long sleeves, ventilation, careful mixing order, labeled containers, and secure storage. Active ferments can build gas pressure, so use breathable covers or vented containers and keep them away from children and pets. Compost teas sprayed on edible crops require special caution: use high-quality finished compost, clean equipment, safe water, careful sanitation, and conservative edible-crop timing, especially if sugars/molasses or manure-derived compost are used. Do not spray questionable brews on harvestable plant parts.

Plant Sap Analysis Preview

Foliar applications are most defensible when guided by data rather than guesswork. The full protocol for collecting plant sap samples and interpreting laboratory analysis results is detailed in Chapter 46.

Figure LSE_C07

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Plant Sap Analysis Preview

Methodology Comparison: Foliar Applications

  • Soil Food Web (Ingham): Soil Food Web practitioners use AACT or compost extracts as biological foliar applications. Potential disease-suppression effects are variable and depend on compost quality, brew/extract method, sanitation, crop, pathogen, weather, and timing.
  • Coot's Mix (Bennett): Coot's Mix practitioners may use foliar kelp or aloe as stress-support tools; claims about SAR, enzymes, or pest resistance should remain context-dependent.
  • Bionutrient (Kittredge): Bionutrient foliar programs are practitioner strategies based on sap analysis and targeted mineral correction, not universal prescriptions.
  • KNF/JADAM (Cho): KNF/JADAM practitioner protocols use highly diluted FPJ, FFJ, WCA, JWA, and related inputs by crop stage. WCA should not be presented as a blossom-end-rot cure; foliar calcium does not reliably correct the water/root-stress physiology behind BER.
  • Biodynamics (Steiner): Biodynamic practice applies Prep 501 (Horn Silica) as a fine mist in the early morning during fruiting and ripening stages. Mechanism not established in peer-reviewed literature; the methodology is surveyed at this level only per Book 1 scope.

North Texas Note: Timing is critical for foliar applications in the Texas summer. By late morning, heat, low humidity, and intense solar radiation can reduce uptake and increase leaf-burn risk. Dawn or very early morning applications are usually safer than midday sprays. Because hard, alkaline water can interfere with some foliar mixes, use clean rainwater, filtered water, or otherwise tested water when the product or protocol calls for low-mineral water.

Chapter 42: Disease Identification and Management

In a living soil system, disease is often viewed as a signal to examine stress, nutrition, airflow, water management, sanitation, and crop genetics. Management should still use the disease triangle: disease develops when a susceptible host, a capable pathogen, and favorable environmental conditions overlap. Correcting the underlying condition matters, but it does not replace resistant varieties, rotation, sanitation, drainage, airflow, vector management, and monitoring.

Fungal Pathogens (The Most Common Threat)

Fungal diseases often thrive where airflow is poor, humidity is high, and leaf wetness persists.

Figure LSE_D03

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Fungal Pathogens (The Most Common Threat)

  • Powdery Mildew: Appears as a white, dusty coating on leaves (common on squash and cucumbers). It can thrive in dry conditions when humidity is high. Management: Increase airflow, reduce plant stress, use resistant varieties where available, avoid excessive nitrogen, and consider label-guided or tested low-risk sprays. LAB or raw milk sprays are sometimes used in organic/practitioner programs to alter the leaf surface environment, but they are not a cure and must be used with edible-crop safety in mind.
  • Downy Mildew: Distinct from powdery mildew, it appears as yellow angular spots on the upper leaf surface with purplish-gray fuzz underneath. Thrives in cool, wet conditions. Management: Improve airflow, avoid overhead watering, remove heavily infected tissue, use resistant varieties where available, and use copper or other products only as a label-guided last resort because repeated copper use can harm soil biology and beneficials.
  • Early Blight (Alternaria solani): Causes target-like concentric rings on lower tomato leaves. It is soil-borne and splashes up onto the foliage during rain. Management: Prune lower leaves, apply a thick layer of mulch to reduce soil splash, rotate where possible, remove infected debris, maintain airflow, and treat AACT only as a variable biological foliar tool whose performance depends on compost quality, brew/extract sanitation, pathogen, host, timing, and weather.
  • Septoria Leaf Spot: Causes numerous small, circular spots with dark borders and gray centers on lower tomato leaves. Management: Similar to Early Blight; mulch heavily, prune lower leaves, avoid leaf wetness, remove infected debris, and rotate or relocate susceptible crops where feasible.
  • Anthracnose: Causes sunken, dark, water-soaked lesions on ripe fruit (especially tomatoes and peppers). Management: Harvest fruit promptly, avoid overhead watering, reduce fruit contact with soil, remove infected fruit, and manage canopy airflow and plant stress.

Figure LSE_D15

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Fungal Pathogens (The Most Common Threat)

Figure LSE_D19

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Fungal Pathogens (The Most Common Threat)

Soil-Borne Pathogens (Wilts and Rots)

  • Southern Blight (Sclerotium rolfsii): A serious soil-borne fungus that attacks the base of the stem, causing rapid wilting and death. Characterized by white fungal growth and small, mustard-seed-like sclerotia at the soil line. Management: Remove and destroy the infected plant and nearby contaminated mulch/soil immediately; do not compost it. Solarize where appropriate, improve sanitation, and rotate to non-susceptible crops for several years when feasible.
  • Fusarium Wilt: A soil-borne fungus that enters through the roots and blocks the xylem, causing the plant to wilt (often on one side first) and the vascular tissue to turn brown. Management: Plant resistant varieties (marked with an 'F'), rotate when feasible, avoid moving contaminated soil, manage root stress, and build biologically active soil as one possible contributor to suppression. Do not claim that biology alone competitively excludes Fusarium.
  • Phytophthora Root Rot: Thrives in poorly drained, waterlogged soils. Causes roots to turn dark and mushy, leading to plant collapse. Management: Improve drainage, plant on raised mounds, avoid overwatering, avoid moving contaminated water or soil, and use resistant varieties where available.

Figure LSE_D01

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Soil-Borne Pathogens (Wilts and Rots)

Figure LSE_D02

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Soil-Borne Pathogens (Wilts and Rots)

Bacterial and Viral Pathogens

  • Bacterial Spot/Speck: Causes small, dark, water-soaked spots on leaves and fruit. Management: Remove infected tissue where practical, clean and disinfect tools, avoid overhead watering, avoid working plants while wet, and use label-guided products only when justified.
  • Viruses (e.g., Tobacco Mosaic Virus, Cucumber Mosaic Virus): Often transmitted by insect vectors (like aphids or thrips) or mechanical transfer. Plants exhibit mottled, distorted, or "shoestring" leaves. Management: There is no curative treatment for systemic plant viruses. Remove and destroy infected plants when needed, control vectors through IPM, manage weeds that host viruses or vectors, disinfect tools, and avoid composting infected material when spread risk is high.

Figure LSE_D05

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Bacterial and Viral Pathogens

Figure UC-029 ◆

Placement: inline near target heading: Functional Navigation Guide

Safety Box: Disease Sprays, Compost Tea, Copper, and Botanicals Disease sprays should be selected after identifying the disease and considering the host, pathogen, and environment. Compost tea is not guaranteed disease control and can create food-safety risk if brewed from manure-derived compost, fed with sugars/molasses, handled with dirty equipment, or sprayed on edible plant parts near harvest. Copper, sulfur, oils, soaps, JHS/JWA, milk, LAB, and biological products can injure plants or harm beneficial organisms if misused. Follow product labels or tested protocols, wear appropriate protection, avoid spraying during heat or pollinator activity, and do not use toxic plant extracts casually.

Figure LSE_D16

Placement: inline after 2nd para: Bacterial and Viral Pathogens

Methodology Comparison: Disease Management

  • Soil Food Web (Ingham): Soil Food Web practitioners use AACT or compost extracts to increase leaf-surface or soil biological activity. Disease suppression is variable; it is not accurate to say AACT prevents disease by covering 100% of the leaf surface.
  • Coot's Mix (Bennett): Coot's Mix practitioners often emphasize silica sources such as rice hulls or wollastonite. Silicon and mineral balance may influence plant structure and tolerance, but cell walls should not be described as impenetrable to fungal hyphae.
  • Bionutrient (Kittredge): Bionutrient practitioners interpret disease as a clue that mineral nutrition and sap chemistry may need attention. Nutrition can influence susceptibility, but disease is not a one-to-one mineral diagnosis, and correcting minerals does not guarantee disappearance.
  • KNF/JADAM (Cho): KNF/JADAM practitioner protocols may use JWA and JHS as direct disease-management tools and IMO-style inputs to support soil biology. These should be used within IPM thresholds and safety controls, especially when KOH or toxic botanicals are involved.
  • Biodynamics (Steiner): Biodynamic practice applies Prep 508 (Equisetum/Horsetail tea) as a preventative spray in biodynamic doctrine. Mechanism not established in peer-reviewed literature; the methodology is surveyed at this level only per Book 1 scope.

North Texas Note: Southern Blight (Sclerotium rolfsii) is a serious North Texas problem during the peak heat of July and August. It thrives in hot, humid soils and can kill susceptible tomato and pepper plants quickly. If you see white mycelium and mustard-seed-like sclerotia at the base of a dying plant, carefully bag the plant and contaminated material around the crown, dispose of it outside the compost system, and clean tools, gloves, and boots before moving to another bed.

Chapter 43: Brix Monitoring as a Trend Indicator

Brix is a measurement of soluble solids in a liquid, expressed primarily as a refractive-index estimate. In plant sap, sugars are usually the dominant contributor, while minerals, amino acids, and other compounds may also influence the reading. A refractometer can be useful for trend monitoring, but Brix is not a stand-alone diagnostic for nutrient density, pest resistance, or disease resistance.

Figure UC-025 ◆

Placement: inline near target heading: Chapter 43: Brix Monitoring as a Trend Indicator

The Science of Brix

A refractometer measures how light bends (refracts) as it passes through the plant sap. The higher the concentration of soluble solids, the more the light bends, resulting in a higher Brix reading.

  • Low Brix: May indicate low soluble solids under the sampling conditions used. It can be associated with stress, low photosynthetic output, dilution after irrigation or rain, immature tissue, heat shutdown, or nutrient imbalance, but it does not diagnose the cause by itself.
  • Moderate Brix: Indicates an intermediate soluble-solids reading under comparable sampling conditions. Interpretation requires crop, cultivar, time of day, leaf age, weather, and recent irrigation context.
  • High Brix: May reflect stronger soluble-solid accumulation and photosynthetic performance under comparable sampling conditions. In the Kempf/Bionutrient practitioner framework, higher readings are associated with improved crop quality and resilience, but they should not be presented as pest immunity or as proof that insects cannot digest the plant.

Brix as a Monitoring Tool

Regular Brix readings taken under consistent conditions can reveal trends before visual stress is obvious, but they should be interpreted alongside scouting, weather, irrigation history, soil or sap testing, and plant symptoms. A falling Brix trend may prompt closer observation or a test-guided foliar application, but it does not reliably predict an aphid outbreak or justify automatic spraying.

The Protocol Preview

Taking a useful Brix reading requires consistency. The sap should be extracted from the same part of the plant, ideally from comparable leaves at the same time of day, with notes on weather, irrigation, crop stage, and heat stress. The full, step-by-step protocol for using a refractometer and interpreting the results is detailed in Chapter 47 (Diagnostics and Measurement).

North Texas Note: During extreme August heat, many plants reduce gas exchange to conserve water, and a reading taken at 3:00 PM on a 105°F day may not be comparable to a reading taken in mild weather. During heat waves, take readings at a consistent earlier window, such as mid-morning, and interpret the number as a trend rather than a pest-resistance score.

    • Added a forward reference to Ch 46 in Ch 41.
