Cover crops suppress weeds by canopy closure, residue, root competition, and sometimes allelopathy. Stale seedbed technique intentionally germinates a flush of weed seeds in the top layer, kills them with minimal disturbance, and then plants the crop.

Seedbank management is the long game. Every weed that sets seed increases future labor. Every clean harvest window that prevents seed return lowers future pressure. Perennial weed management is different. Bermuda grass, nutsedge, johnsongrass, bindweed, and thistle-like perennials are not solved by one shallow pass; fragments and underground storage organs can regrow.

Weed management does NOT mean every non-crop plant is bad. Managed path vegetation, insectary strips, cover crops, and living mulch can be useful. It also does NOT mean one tactic controls all weeds. Mulch helps annual weeds but may not stop nutsedge. Cultivation helps annual seedlings but spreads rhizome fragments if done poorly. Herbicides, where used, are label-governed tools, not soil-health practices.

How to Use It

Use a layered program.

  1. Prevent seed rain. Pull, cut, mow, or tarp weeds before flowering and seed set.
  2. Keep beds covered. Use mulch after seeding or transplant establishment. Keep paths covered with wood chips, living cover, or managed mulch.
  3. Use stale seedbed before direct-seeded crops. Prepare the surface, irrigate or wait for rain, allow weed flush, kill emerged weeds shallowly, then plant with minimal disturbance.
  4. Cultivate shallowly and early. Small weeds die easily; large weeds cost roots and labor. Scrape the top soil, do not dig deeply around vegetable roots.
  5. Hand-weed in crop rows. Use hands or narrow tools near stems and drip lines.
  6. Use drip irrigation. Wet the crop row, not the whole weed seedbank.
  7. Use cover crops for idle windows. Select species and termination method before planting.
  8. Edge the garden. Perennial weeds invade from lawn and path edges.
  9. Remove problem perennials deliberately. Dig, smother, solarize/occult, or use label-supported herbicide where the system allows.
  10. Record problem species and timing. Next year’s plan starts from this year’s seedbank.

Pre-emergence herbicide logic belongs only where the product is labeled for the crop, site, timing, and grower’s standards. Corn gluten meal is sometimes marketed as a natural pre-emergent, but effectiveness is context-dependent and it adds nitrogen; do not treat it as a universal weed solution.

Interactions and Pairings

Weed management interacts with soil pedology. Wet Blackland clay should not be cultivated; wait for friable moisture or use surface mulch/tarping. It interacts with irrigation. Overhead irrigation wets the whole surface and germinates more weeds; drip narrows the germination zone. It interacts with mulch and nitrogen. High-carbon mulch on the surface suppresses weeds and protects soil; mixing it into the root zone can immobilize nitrogen.

It interacts with crop density. Strong crop canopy suppresses weeds, but overcrowding can increase disease. It interacts with IPM. Some weeds host pests, while some flowering plants support beneficials. The decision is not “all weeds bad” but “which plants, where, when, and with what risk.”

When to Avoid It

Do not cultivate deeply around established crops. Deep hoeing prunes roots, brings up fresh weed seed, dries soil, and damages structure. Do not leave weeds to flower “for beneficial insects” inside production beds without knowing pest and seed risk. Do not use herbicides without a label for the crop and site. Do not use contaminated hay, manure, or grass clippings as mulch.

Common failures include waiting until weeds are large, stirring the seedbank repeatedly, mulching over perennial weeds without enough suppression, using living mulch too close to young vegetables, and ignoring path edges. Another failure is trying to keep soil bare and polished; bare soil in North Texas bakes, crusts, and loses moisture.

How to Check Your Results

Verify by weed counts, crop vigor, seed return, labor time, and recurrence. A successful program has fewer weed seedlings after each flush, less seed set, easier weeding, fewer perennial escapes, and less crop competition during the critical early growth window. For stale seedbeds, success is visible as a first flush killed before planting and fewer weeds after crop emergence. For mulch, success is fewer light-sensitive annuals and better soil moisture.

If weeds persist, identify species. Grasses, sedges, annual broadleaves, and perennials require different tools. If a bed repeatedly fails, check mulch depth, irrigation pattern, crop spacing, edge invasion, and seed source.

Storage, Shelf Life, and Lifecycle

Weed management follows seedbank cycles. Preventing one season’s seed set can reduce future pressure. Perennial root systems require repeated depletion. Mulch must be renewed as it decomposes. Tarps must be stored dry and protected from UV when not in use. Hoes, knives, and hand tools need cleaning and sharpening. Herbicides, if used, have label shelf life and storage restrictions.

Sourcing and Quality Checks

Source clean mulch, straw, compost, and cover crop seed. Avoid hay with weed seed or herbicide risk. Check cover crop seed labels for weed seed and hard seed. Choose tools that match bed width: stirrup hoes for paths and open rows, collinear hoes for shallow work, knives for tight spaces, and hand pulling near crops. For tarps, use opaque material heavy enough to exclude light and withstand wind.

For any herbicide or pre-emergent, verify crop/site label, active ingredient, PHI/REI where relevant, rate, temperature limits, irrigation requirements, and local legality.

North Texas Growing Note

North Texas weed pressure follows moisture pulses. Warm rain after drought produces flushes. Summer irrigation can grow weeds faster than crops if drip is poorly placed. Blackland clay makes timing critical: too wet, and tools smear; too dry, and weeds snap off without roots. Mulch before June heat and keep paths covered. Bermuda grass and nutsedge require edge control and repeated depletion; one mulch layer is not enough. Fall beds need stale seedbed or shallow flush control before carrots, lettuce, spinach, cilantro, and brassicas.

For final use, pair this entry with a weed-family and life-cycle quick chart. The chart should distinguish seedling annuals, established annuals, rhizomatous perennials, sedges, and volunteer crops, because the tool choice changes by category.

This chart should be practical, not botanical trivia: the purpose is to choose the least disruptive effective tactic.

Related Topics

Mulch Types and Selection; Cover Crop Planting and Termination SOP; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; Crop Rotation by Family; Soil Pedology Basics; Persistent Herbicide Contamination; Corn Gluten Meal; IPM Hierarchy; Insectary Guild Design; M1-1: No-Till Bed Establishment.

LSE-192 — Insectary Guild Design

Insectary guild design is the planned use of flowering, sheltering, and seasonally sequenced plants to support beneficial insects around production beds. It overlaps with insectary planting, beneficial-insect habitat, flowering strips, pollinator strips, conservation biological control, and beneficial borders. The functional goal is simple: keep nectar, pollen, refuge, alternate prey, and seasonal continuity close enough to crops that predators and parasitoids are present before pest populations exceed thresholds.

An insectary guild is not one magic plant. It is a functional planting with several resource types. Adult hoverflies, parasitoid wasps, tachinid flies, lacewings, lady beetles, minute pirate bugs, bees, and other beneficials may use nectar or pollen. Ground beetles, spiders, and other predators need shelter, low disturbance, and overwintering cover. Some natural enemies also need alternate prey when the target pest is scarce. A guild works by improving the odds that these organisms can live near the garden long enough to matter.

The most useful plant groups usually have accessible flowers, a reliable bloom window, and manageable growth. Umbels such as dill, cilantro, fennel, parsley flowers, carrot flowers, and related Apiaceae are valuable because many tiny parasitoid wasps and small flies can use shallow exposed flowers. Composites such as yarrow, coreopsis, cosmos, sunflower, black-eyed Susan, asters, goldenrod, and coneflower can support a range of insects. Mints and culinary herbs such as basil, thyme, oregano, sage, and mountain mint can add bloom and scent diversity. Legumes such as cowpea flowers, clover, vetch, and native prairie legumes add floral resources and, in the right context, soil functions. Native and adapted perennials can bridge heat gaps if they match the site.

Flower form matters. Deep, double ornamental blooms may be beautiful but less useful for the small parasitoids and flies that need exposed nectaries. Timing matters even more. A spring flush of cilantro and dill does not help August spider mites if nothing is blooming later. Distance matters: biological-control benefits often weaken as habitat gets farther from the crop. Pesticide compatibility matters most of all. Broad-spectrum insecticides, careless oils, or hot-weather sprays can erase the natural enemies the guild is meant to support.

The editorial boundary is important: insectary guilds support IPM; they do not replace it. They increase the probability that natural enemies are present and effective. They do not guarantee pest suppression. They also do not prove companion-planting folklore. “Yarrow repels all pests,” “basil protects tomatoes,” or “flowers prevent squash bugs” are doctrine unless the pest, plant, timing, and mechanism are specified.

Design starts with the pest window. Identify the recurring pests in the garden: aphids in cool-season crops, caterpillars in brassicas and tomatoes, cucumber beetles and squash bugs in cucurbits, spider mites in heat, whiteflies and thrips in summer and fall, or harlequin bugs in brassicas. Then choose plants that flower before and during those windows. A useful garden usually needs at least three bloom bands. Early bloom might come from cilantro, dill, parsley flowers, calendula, alyssum where brassica-family risk is acceptable, and early perennials. Summer bloom might come from basil, cosmos, zinnia, cowpea flowers, coreopsis, mountain mint, black-eyed Susan, sages, and heat-adapted natives. Late bloom might come from goldenrod, asters, late herbs, frost-tolerant flowers, and fall annuals.

Placement is a design decision, not decoration. In small gardens, one managed strip near several beds is often better than scattering aggressive perennials inside annual rows. Place guilds at bed ends, pathway edges, fence lines, perennial borders, trellis bases, or dedicated strips. Keep scouting and harvest access open. Keep irrigation zones compatible. Keep tall or dense flowers away from disease-prone crops where they block light or airflow. Keep perennials outside annual turnover zones unless containment is planned.

A good guild also has to be scouted. An aphid colony with parasitoid mummies and hoverfly larvae is not the same decision as an aphid colony with no natural enemies. A sunflower that concentrates leaf-footed bugs may be useful only if the grower monitors it and removes or manages the pests before they spill over. A flower strip full of thrips near peppers may become a liability. Biological control is still management.

When sprays are needed, protect the beneficials first. Use pest identification, thresholds, and the least disruptive tool that fits. Avoid spraying open flowers during pollinator activity. Do not use broad-spectrum products across the guild and then expect resident natural enemies. Project safety boundaries still apply: neem oil is not applied above 85°F, and wettable sulfur within two weeks of oil is a severe phytotoxicity risk.

Results should be verified by scouting both the guild and the crop. Record bloom species, bloom dates, beneficial insects observed, pest counts, natural-enemy activity, and crop injury. Look for parasitoid mummies, hoverfly larvae, lacewing eggs, lady beetle larvae, minute pirate bugs, tachinid flies, predatory wasps, spiders, ground beetles, and predatory mites where relevant. A guild that helps with aphids may do nothing for squash vine borer. If pests exceed thresholds, escalate through IPM. The guild supports the decision; it is not the decision.

North Texas growing note: North Texas insectary design has to bridge spring abundance, summer heat, and fall resurgence. Cilantro and dill can be useful early, but they bolt quickly and will not carry July. Summer guilds need heat-tolerant plants, mulch, and targeted irrigation. Native or adapted perennials should be established in fall or spring rather than peak heat. Blackland clay often favors permanent edges and paths instead of constantly reworked flower strips inside annual beds. Yarrow is a useful managed example because of drought tolerance and accessible flowers, but it is not a magic pest-control plant. The final image/bloom-calendar workflow should separate pollinator value from predator and parasitoid support, because bee flowers are not automatically the best flowers for tiny parasitic wasps.

Related: manuscript anchors: IPM Hierarchy; Scouting Protocol; Daily Scouting SOP; Beneficial Predators; Companion Planting (Mechanistic Frame); Crop Rotation by Family; Cut Flowers as Functional Cover; Culinary Herb Integration; Weed Management; Aphids; Cucumber Beetles; Squash Bugs.

Chapter — Herbs in the North Texas Living Soil Garden: Growing, Harvesting, Processing, and Storing Culinary Herbs

Controlled chapter module. This is not a canonical encyclopedia entry and is not publication locked. It is integrated as a chapter-level manuscript module under v1.9 production control.

1. Chapter Purpose

Herbs are often treated as decorative extras, but in a North Texas living-soil garden they are working crops. They provide kitchen harvests, insectary bloom, perennial edges, pollinator and natural-enemy resources, pest-scouting indicators, seasonal transition markers, and small-space productivity. They also reveal whether the grower understands microclimate. Cilantro, dill, parsley, basil, rosemary, thyme, mint, fennel, oregano, sage, lavender, lemongrass, bay, chives, garlic chives, Mexican mint marigold, epazote, and lemon balm do not want the same soil, water, light, harvest timing, or storage method.

This chapter does not treat “herbs” as one crop. A single “herb bed” with one irrigation schedule is one of the most common reasons herbs fail. In North Texas, herbs must be grouped by growth season, root behavior, water demand, drainage tolerance, heat tolerance, harvest stage, and postharvest handling. Basil is a warm-season leaf crop and is chilling-sensitive after harvest. Cilantro is a cool-season herb that bolts when heat rises. Dill is direct-seeded and short-lived. Rosemary, thyme, oregano, sage, and lavender demand drainage. Mint demands containment. Lemongrass demands heat, water, and frost planning. Bay laurel demands correct identification.

Texas A&M distinguishes herbs from spices by use: herbs are commonly fresh cut leaves or soft plant parts, while spices are often processed seed, bark, root, or similar plant parts. That distinction matters because basil leaves, cilantro leaves, coriander seed, dill seed, fennel bulb, rosemary stems, thyme sprigs, bay leaves, and lemongrass stalks are grown, harvested, processed, and stored differently.

The goal of this chapter is operational: decide where each herb belongs, how to grow it in North Texas, when to harvest it, how to process it without destroying quality, and what storage method protects the crop. This chapter does not make medicinal claims, does not give dosage advice, does not discuss essential-oil therapeutic use, and does not treat herbs as substitutes for scouting, crop rotation, irrigation, sanitation, or IPM.

2. North Texas Herb Design Principles

Principle 1 — Separate dry herbs from moist herbs

North Texas Blackland clay makes drainage management the first herb decision. Rosemary, thyme, oregano, marjoram, sage, lavender, and bay laurel want sharp drainage and moderate fertility. Mint, parsley, cilantro, basil, chives, garlic chives, Vietnamese coriander, shiso, and lemongrass want more consistent moisture. Putting all of them into one bed forces at least one group into the wrong conditions.

A dry herb strip should be raised, well-drained, lightly mulched, and watered deeply but less often once plants are established. It should not receive the same emitter schedule as basil, parsley, cilantro, or mint. A moist kitchen herb bed should have drip irrigation, organic matter, surface mulch after establishment, and seasonal replanting. A containment/insectary edge should hold mint, lemon balm, garlic chives, yarrow, fennel, oregano flowers, basil flowers, cilantro flowers, dill flowers, Mexican mint marigold, and other flowering herbs where spread or reseeding can be managed.

Principle 2 — Grow herbs by season, not by recipe

Cilantro, dill, parsley, fennel, chervil, and many leafy herbs are cool-season or shoulder-season herbs. Basil, Thai basil, lemon basil, holy basil, lemongrass, epazote, Vietnamese coriander, and shiso are warm-season herbs. Rosemary, thyme, oregano, sage, chives, mint, bay, lavender, lemon balm, and Mexican mint marigold are perennial or semi-perennial herbs with species-specific winter behavior.

Cilantro is the clearest example. Texas A&M states that cilantro grows best at 50–85°F, tolerates cold, and starts bolting when temperatures exceed 85°F; it recommends February planting for April harvest and September planting for November harvest in Texas. That means a North Texas grower should not fight cilantro through July. The correct summer replacements are basil, Thai basil, epazote, lemongrass, shiso, Vietnamese coriander, and other heat-season herbs.

Principle 3 — Harvest stage determines quality

Herbs are not harvested at one universal stage. Basil is kept vegetative by cutting above nodes and removing flower spikes. Cilantro leaves are harvested before bolting, while coriander seed is harvested after seed maturity. Dill fronds are harvested before flowering, while dill seed is harvested after seed heads mature. Thyme, oregano, sage, and rosemary are often harvested before or around bloom depending on use. Lemongrass is harvested by stalk. Bay leaves are harvested mature. Mint and lemon balm are cut repeatedly to keep tender regrowth.

A grower who harvests all herbs “whenever needed” will get uneven quality. Harvest too late and cilantro becomes coriander, basil becomes woody and floral, dill becomes seed, mint becomes coarse, and lavender loses the tight bud stage. Harvest too hard and rosemary, thyme, lavender, and sage lose recovery capacity in heat. Harvest stage is a production decision, not a kitchen afterthought.

Principle 4 — Storage method is crop-specific

The most important postharvest rule in this chapter is that basil is not stored like most other herbs. UC Davis states that most fresh culinary herbs store best near 32°F, but basil and shiso are chilling-sensitive and should be stored above 50°F. UC ANR similarly lists thyme, oregano, rosemary, mints, sage, parsley, cilantro, savory, marjoram, dill, and tarragon as herbs whose optimum postharvest temperature is about 32°F, with shelf life of 3–4 weeks possible under proper conditions.

Practical rule:

  • Basil and shiso: store above 50°F.
  • Most other fresh herbs: store cold, humid, and clean.
  • Woody herbs: dry well and store whole.
  • Tender herbs: use fresh, freeze, or dry quickly.
  • Seed herbs: dry fully, thresh, and store sealed.
  • Stalk herbs: refrigerate short term or freeze for long storage.

Herb preservation in oil is a food-safety topic, not a casual storage method. Herbs and vegetables in oil can create botulism risk if handled incorrectly; homemade infused oils require extension-tested acidification/refrigeration guidance and must not be treated as ordinary pantry storage.

Principle 5 — Insectary use is habitat support, not pest magic

Flowering herbs can support natural enemies by providing nectar, pollen, shelter, and sometimes alternate prey. UC IPM describes insectary plants as plants that provide nectar, pollen, shelter, and alternate prey for natural enemies and pollinators. That supports herbs as part of IPM design. It does not mean herbs repel pests universally or eliminate scouting. Companion-planting claims remain practitioner doctrine unless the mechanism, target pest, timing, and evidence are specific.

3. Herb Bed Systems

A. Dry Mediterranean Herb Strip

Suitable herbs: rosemary, thyme, oregano, sweet marjoram, sage, lavender, winter savory, and bay laurel in a nearby container.

This bed should be the highest and best-drained herb zone. In Blackland clay, use a raised strip, berm, stone-edged bed, gravelly container cluster, or sharply drained edge. Do not put this group in a low bed or a heavily composted moisture-retentive bed. Add compost only enough to improve tilth; do not create a rich wet sponge. Use drip irrigation, but keep the emitter schedule separate from basil, mint, parsley, cilantro, and lemongrass.

The surface can be mulched, but avoid burying woody crowns. Coarse mulch, pea gravel, stone mulch, or very shallow coarse organic mulch can work better than deep fine compost around rosemary, lavender, thyme, and sage. These herbs need oxygen around the crown and roots. Texas A&M’s herb landscape guidance warns that lavender, rosemary, sage, and thyme grow best when leaves stay mostly dry and that lingering moisture encourages fungal disease.

What this bed does NOT do: a Mediterranean herb strip does not replace crop rotation for vegetable beds. It is a perennial edge or dedicated herb zone. It also does not need constant fertility. Overwatering and overfeeding are more common failures than starvation.

Processing and storage from this bed: woody herbs are often best dried rather than refrigerated for long storage. Harvest stems in the morning after dew dries. Rinse only if necessary; wet herbs dry more slowly and can mold. Tie small bundles or lay stems on screens in shade with airflow. Once leaves are crisp, strip leaves from stems and store whole leaves in airtight jars away from heat and light. Crush only when using; crushed herbs lose aroma faster.

B. Moist Kitchen Herb Bed

Suitable herbs: basil, cilantro, dill, parsley, chives, garlic chives, Florence fennel, shiso/perilla, Vietnamese coriander, cutting celery, leaf fennel, and seasonal edible flowers.

This bed is seasonal. In fall and winter it holds cilantro, parsley, dill, fennel, chives, and garlic chives. In late spring and summer it pivots to basil, Thai basil, lemon basil, holy basil, shiso, Vietnamese coriander, lemongrass, and epazote. It should have drip irrigation, a fine seedbed for small-seeded herbs, light mulch after seedlings establish, and enough organic matter to buffer moisture without becoming anaerobic.

This bed can be richer than the Mediterranean strip, but fertility still needs restraint. Too much nitrogen produces soft basil, aphid-prone cilantro, floppy dill, and weak parsley. Compost, moderate organic fertility, and steady moisture are better than repeated high-nitrogen pushes. Do not use this bed as a dumping place for unfinished compost, fresh manure, or high-salt inputs.

What this bed does NOT do: a moist herb bed does not stay in one planting all year. It is a bed-pivot system. Cilantro exits when heat rises; basil enters when soil warms. Basil exits at frost; cilantro and dill return in fall. Parsley may bridge the seasons but usually declines as summer stress builds.

Processing and storage from this bed: tender herbs are processed quickly. Remove field heat, sort out yellow or damaged leaves, and store by crop. Cilantro, parsley, dill, chives, mint, and lemon balm can be refrigerated. Basil should not go into a cold refrigerator; hold it above 50°F, often with stems in water and a loose bag over the top for short-term use. For longer storage, freeze basil as pesto-style cubes, chopped leaves, or herb cubes rather than relying on drying if fresh flavor is the goal. Any oil-based freezing or preservation method should follow extension food-safety guidance.

C. Containment and Insectary Edge

Suitable herbs: mint, lemon balm, yarrow, fennel, garlic chives, oregano flowers, thyme flowers, basil flowers on selected plants, cilantro/coriander flowers, dill flowers, parsley flowers, Mexican mint marigold, mountain mint where adapted, and other managed flowering herbs.

This is not the main annual vegetable bed. It is an edge, container row, path border, pollinator strip, or permanent guild zone. Use it to keep aggressive herbs out of rotation beds while still benefiting from harvest and flowers. Mint and lemon balm belong here or in containers, not in a vegetable rotation bed. Fennel belongs here if the goal is seed and insectary bloom; Florence fennel grown for bulbs may belong in a cool-season kitchen bed but should still be tracked because it is large and competitive.

Insectary herbs should bloom in sequence. Spring bloom from cilantro, dill, parsley, and chives is useful but does not feed beneficial insects in July. Summer basil, oregano, mint, yarrow, coreopsis, zinnia, and native flowers extend resources. Fall garlic chives, Mexican mint marigold, basil, asters, and goldenrod continue the sequence. The planting should be scouted as carefully as the crop bed because it can also host aphids, caterpillars, mites, or reseeding problems.

What this zone does NOT do: an insectary edge does not eliminate pest scouting. It does not validate every companion-planting claim. The mechanism is habitat support, not universal pest repulsion.

D. Containers

Containers are not only for patios. In North Texas they are the safest way to grow mint, lemon balm, bay laurel, lemongrass for overwintering, rosemary in wet yards, and trial lavender in heavy clay. Containers give drainage control, root containment, portability, and frost management. They also dry quickly in heat, accumulate salts faster than beds, and depend on regular irrigation.

Use large containers for woody perennials. Small pots overheat and dry too fast. Mint can survive in smaller pots but performs better in a wide, heavy container. Lemongrass needs a large pot or it becomes root-bound and dries daily in summer. Bay laurel is slow-growing and container-friendly, but the pot must drain and the plant must be protected in severe cold. Container soil should be well-drained, not garden clay. A saucer that holds water under rosemary, thyme, sage, lavender, or bay is a failure mode, not a moisture reserve.

4. Herb-by-Herb Production, Processing, and Storage

Basil

Best North Texas role: Basil is the main summer leaf herb. It belongs in the warm-season kitchen herb bed, large containers, or annual herb rows after frost. It pairs well with drip irrigation, mulch, regular pruning, and succession planting.

Growing preferences: Basil likes warm soil, warm nights, full sun to light afternoon relief, steady moisture, and good drainage. It should not be transplanted into cold spring soil. Wait until frost danger has passed and soil is warm. A practical North Texas outdoor planting window is mid-April through summer, with fall transplants only where frost timing allows. Grow basil 10–18 inches apart depending on cultivar. Compact globe types can be closer; Genovese and Thai basil need more space. Use drip irrigation or water at soil level. Avoid overhead evening watering where downy mildew has been a problem.

Planting / propagation notes: Basil can be direct-seeded after soil warms, but transplants are usually more reliable for first plantings. Pinch early to encourage branching. Succession plant if the kitchen needs continuous young leaves. Do not rely on old woody basil plants to carry the entire season.

Harvest timing: Begin cutting once plants are established and have several leaf pairs. Cut above a node, leaving at least two sets of leaves below the cut. Remove flower spikes as soon as they form if leaf production is the goal. If insectary function is desired, allow only selected plants to flower and keep culinary plants vegetative. Basil does not keep producing high-quality leaves if allowed to flower unchecked.

Processing: For fresh use, harvest in the morning after dew dries. Do not bruise leaves. Remove damaged leaves and woody stems. Do not wash until use unless leaves are dusty; if washing, dry thoroughly. For freezing, chop basil or puree with oil for pesto-style storage, then freeze in small portions and follow food-safety guidance. Do not rely on dried basil for the same flavor as fresh basil; drying changes aroma.

Storage: Basil is chilling-sensitive. Store above 50°F, not in a standard cold refrigerator. UC Davis states that basil and shiso should be stored above 50°F and can have 1–2 weeks shelf life depending on water loss. For home use, hold stems in a jar of water at room temperature, loosely cover the leaves, and change water as needed. Do not seal wet basil in plastic.

North Texas failure modes: planting too early into cold soil; letting flower spikes dominate; overhead irrigation during humid weather; cold refrigerator storage; downy mildew on susceptible varieties; spider mites during hot dry stress.

Variety / cultivar notes: Genovese and Italian Large Leaf are standard culinary types. Thai basil, holy basil, lemon basil, purple basil, and globe basil fill different culinary and ornamental roles. For downy mildew pressure, consider DMR cultivars such as Rutgers Devotion DMR, Obsession DMR, Passion DMR, Thunderstruck DMR, Prospera, Prospera Compact DMR, and Amazel where available.

Cilantro / Coriander

Best North Texas role: Cilantro is a fall, winter, and early-spring herb. Coriander is the mature seed from the same plant. It belongs in the cool-season herb bed, not the summer basil bed.

Growing preferences: Cilantro prefers cool weather, consistent moisture, and direct seeding. Texas A&M states that cilantro does best between 50°F and 85°F and bolts when temperatures exceed 85°F; it recommends February planting for April harvest and September planting for November harvest. Sow shallowly, about ¼ inch deep, in rows or small patches. Succession sow every 1–2 weeks during the cool season for continuous harvest. Keep soil evenly moist during germination. Afternoon shade can help in warm shoulder periods, but it does not make cilantro a summer crop.

Planting / propagation notes: Direct seed rather than transplanting large starts. Cilantro has a taproot and resents disturbance. Seed can be split or lightly crushed only if the seed lot/germination practice supports it; do not overprocess seed.

Harvest timing: For leaf harvest, cut outer stems when plants are 4–6 inches tall, or shear the plant above the growing point for regrowth. Harvest before the central stem elongates. Once bolting begins, leaf quality declines. For coriander seed, let plants flower and set seed. Harvest seed heads when they brown and dry. Finish drying in a paper bag or on a screen, then thresh and winnow.

Processing: Fresh cilantro is best used fresh or frozen. Wash gently, spin or pat dry, and remove yellow leaves. For freezing, chop leaves and stems and freeze in small portions with water or oil depending on intended use and food-safety guidance. Drying cilantro is possible but flavor loss is significant. Coriander seed should be fully dry before storage; store whole seeds in a sealed jar in a cool, dark, dry place.

Storage: Fresh cilantro stores best cold and humid. Trim stems, place in a jar with a little water, loosely cover, and refrigerate, or wrap in a slightly damp towel inside a loose bag. Most non-basil herbs store best near 32°F under proper humidity according to UC ANR.

North Texas failure modes: trying to grow cilantro in summer; not succession sowing; letting plants dry during establishment; treating bolting as a fertility issue; planting too late in spring.

Variety / cultivar notes: Santo, Leisure, Long Standing, Calypso, and Slo Bolt are common choices. Slow-bolt varieties extend the window but do not defeat North Texas summer heat.

Dill

Best North Texas role: Dill is a short-lived cool-to-mild-season herb grown for fronds, flowers, seed heads, and beneficial-insect habitat.

Growing preferences: Dill prefers full sun, well-drained soil, moderate fertility, and open airflow. It is usually direct-seeded because it dislikes transplanting. Tall varieties need wind protection. In North Texas, dill is strongest in spring and fall, not July. Sow seed shallowly and thin plants to reduce crowding.

Planting / propagation notes: Direct seed after hard frost danger has passed for spring dill, or trial fall sowing where a mild fall gives enough time before hard freezes. Sow in small successions rather than one large block. Fernleaf and compact types work better in containers; tall seed-head types need space.

Harvest timing: For dill weed, harvest fronds before flowering for best flavor. Cut individual fronds or small stems without stripping the plant bare. For pickling heads, cut flower heads when seeds are forming but before they shatter. For dry dill seed, allow seed heads to mature and brown, then cut into paper bags and finish drying.

Processing: Fronds can be used fresh, frozen, or dried. Freezing preserves flavor better than drying for many kitchen uses. Chop fronds and freeze in small portions or ice cubes. To dry, use low heat or shade airflow and store in airtight jars. Seed heads should be dried thoroughly, then rubbed or shaken to release seed. Store whole seed in labeled jars away from light and heat.

Storage: Fresh dill stores cold and humid. Wrap in a damp towel in a loose bag or hold stems in water in the refrigerator. UC ANR lists dill among herbs that store best near 32°F, with multi-week shelf life possible under good conditions.

North Texas failure modes: transplanting mature dill; planting in windy exposed beds; letting soil dry during germination; waiting too long for frond harvest; assuming spring dill will carry through July.

Variety / cultivar notes: Bouquet, Dukat, Fernleaf, Long Island, and Superdukat are common. Fernleaf is better for compact spaces; Bouquet and Long Island are better for seed heads and pickling.

Fennel

Best North Texas role: Fennel can be grown as common fennel for fronds/seed, bronze fennel for ornamental/insectary use, or Florence fennel for the swollen bulb-like base.

Growing preferences: Fennel needs full sun, good drainage, and steady moisture. Utah State Extension emphasizes that Florence fennel is different from common fennel and that common fennel does not bulb. Florence fennel grows best under shorter days and cooler temperatures; hot summer maturity produces poor bulbs. Florence fennel especially needs even moisture because drought and heat encourage bolting and woody bulbs.

Planting / propagation notes: Sow seed about ¼ inch deep when soil temperatures are in a moderate range; thin Florence fennel more widely after establishment. Use fall as the main North Texas target for bulb fennel. Common or bronze fennel can live as a managed perennial/edge plant where winter allows, but it can reseed and compete.

Harvest timing: For fronds, harvest lightly throughout growth. For Florence fennel, harvest when the bulb is firm and before it becomes woody or sends up a flower stalk. Utah State gives firm bulbs under 4 inches across as a harvest indicator. For seed, allow umbels to mature and dry, then cut into paper bags and finish drying.

Processing: Fennel fronds are best fresh or frozen. Bulbs are used fresh and refrigerated. Seeds should be dried fully, cleaned, and stored whole in sealed jars.

Storage: Fennel bulbs store refrigerated in a produce bag or container. Fronds wilt quickly and should be used soon, wrapped cold and humid. Seeds store dry, sealed, cool, and dark.

North Texas failure modes: spring planting too late, causing bolting; inconsistent moisture during bulb formation; planting common fennel when Florence fennel was intended; allowing common fennel to reseed; putting tall fennel in the center of a small vegetable bed.

Variety / cultivar notes: Florence fennel cultivars should be chosen for bulb production and bolt resistance. Common fennel and bronze fennel are not substitutes when the desired harvest is the bulb.

Parsley

Best North Texas role: Parsley is a cool-season biennial leaf herb grown as an annual or overwintered crop. It is useful in fall, winter, and spring.

Growing preferences: Parsley prefers cool weather, full sun to light shade, steady moisture, moderate fertility, and well-drained soil. It germinates slowly from seed, so transplants are often practical. Plant in fall for the best North Texas production, or in late winter for spring harvest. Space plants 8–12 inches apart. Parsley does not want the same dry, lean conditions as thyme or rosemary. It belongs in the moist kitchen herb bed.

Planting / propagation notes: Seed can be slow and uneven. Use fresh seed, keep the seedbed consistently moist, and do not allow crusting. Transplants are useful when a gardener needs a faster stand. Treat parsley as Apiaceae in rotation and insectary planning.

Harvest timing: Harvest outer stems from the base, leaving the center growing. Do not clip only leaf tips; take full stems. Harvest before the plant sends a seed stalk. In the second year, flowering stems support beneficial insects, but leaf quality declines.

Processing: Parsley can be used fresh, frozen, or dried. Fresh parsley should be washed, dried, and refrigerated. For freezing, chop and freeze in cubes or flat bags. For drying, use low heat or shade airflow, then store whole flakes or leaves in sealed jars.

Storage: Fresh parsley stores cold and humid. Place stems in water in the refrigerator or wrap in a damp towel in a loose bag. UC ANR lists parsley among herbs suited to 32°F storage.

North Texas failure modes: slow germination mistaken for seed failure; summer decline from heat; overcrowding and aphids; letting second-year flowering eliminate leaf quality; heavy wet mulch around crowns.

Variety / cultivar notes: Flat-leaf parsley is often preferred for kitchen use; curly parsley is useful for garnish and can be sturdy in beds.

Chives

Best North Texas role: Chives are clumping perennial alliums useful for edges, containers, kitchen harvest, and spring bloom.

Growing preferences: Grow in full sun to light afternoon shade with moderate water and good drainage. Chives tolerate cool weather and can go semi-dormant in heat. They like more moisture than rosemary but less wetness than mint. Divide clumps every few years to restore vigor.

Planting / propagation notes: Chives can be started from seed, divisions, or nursery transplants. Divisions are fastest. Keep clumps weeded because fine leaves are difficult to clean once grassy weeds enter. Track them as alliums if allium diseases appear in the garden.

Harvest timing: Cut leaves near the base with scissors, leaving enough growth for regrowth. Harvest flowers for edible garnish only when identity and food use are certain. Remove spent flowers if reseeding is not wanted.

Processing: Use fresh for best quality. Chives lose flavor when dried. Freeze chopped chives in small bags or ice cube trays.

Storage: Fresh chives store refrigerated, wrapped in a damp towel or in a sealed container after drying surface moisture. Use within a week for best flavor. Frozen chives are best used in cooked dishes.

North Texas failure modes: crowded clumps, grass intrusion, rust or allium disease in humid/crowded plantings, treating chives as unrelated to onion/garlic rotation, and allowing the clump to sit in a hot dry container without water.

Garlic Chives

Best North Texas role: Garlic chives are a heat-tolerant perennial allium edge plant with edible leaves and insectary bloom.

Growing preferences: Garlic chives prefer full sun to light shade, moderate water, and well-drained soil. They tolerate heat better than common chives. They form clumps but can reseed aggressively if seed heads mature.

Planting / propagation notes: Start from divisions or transplants. Place where the clump can remain for several seasons. Do not let garlic chives spread into the main rotation bed if allium diseases or reseeding are concerns.

Harvest timing: Cut leaves near the base as needed. Harvest before leaves become coarse. Remove flower stalks if the goal is leaf production; allow some bloom only where insectary function is intentional.

Processing: Use fresh or freeze chopped leaves. Drying is usually disappointing. Flower buds and flowers can be used culinarily where identity and food use are certain.

Storage: Store cold and humid like chives. Freeze for longer storage.

North Texas failure modes: reseeding freely; crowded clumps; rust; using flowering plants as unmanaged weeds; treating the plant as unrelated to garlic/onion disease history.

Mint

Best North Texas role: Mint is a moisture-loving container or containment herb. It is excellent for fresh harvest but aggressive in beds.

Growing preferences: Mint prefers consistent moisture, richer soil, and full sun to afternoon shade. In North Texas summer, afternoon shade improves leaf quality. Grow in containers, lined beds, or dedicated wet corners. Do not plant mint uncontained in a rotation bed. Mint does not respect bed boundaries. Rhizomes and runners spread under mulch, into paths, and through neighboring crops.

Planting / propagation notes: Start from transplants or divisions, not seed if a named flavor type matters. Container culture is the default. Keep containers on hardscape or saucers that do not allow roots to escape into soil. If pots are sunk in beds, inspect drainage holes and runners.

Harvest timing: Cut stems regularly to keep tender regrowth. Harvest before flowering for best leaf quality, or allow controlled flowering for pollinators. Cut back hard if plants become woody or mite-infested, then regrow under good moisture.

Processing: Mint can be used fresh, dried, or frozen. For drying, harvest clean stems, air-dry in shade, strip leaves when crisp, and store whole. For freezing, chop leaves and freeze in cubes. Mint also infuses into culinary syrups or teas, but any preservation method involving oil or sealed low-acid mixtures must follow food-safety guidance.

Storage: Fresh mint stores refrigerated, either as stems in water or wrapped cold and humid. UC ANR lists mints among herbs suited to cold storage.

North Texas failure modes: invasiveness; spider mites in hot dry containers; rust disease; roots escaping through container drainage holes into beds; poor leaf quality in full reflected heat; overgrown containers that dry out daily.

Variety / cultivar notes: Spearmint and peppermint behave differently in kitchen use. Apple mint, chocolate mint, and other named mints should be grown from divisions/transplants to maintain type.

Oregano

Best North Texas role: Oregano is a perennial dry herb for raised edges, Mediterranean strips, containers, and insectary bloom.

Growing preferences: Oregano prefers sun, drainage, moderate to low fertility, and restrained water. It tolerates heat better than many herbs once established. Plant in raised soil, containers, or bed edges. Avoid wet crowns. Oregano does not need rich wet soil. Overfeeding and overwatering reduce quality and increase disease risk.

Planting / propagation notes: Transplants or divisions are more reliable than seed for culinary type. Keep oregano out of the moist kitchen herb bed. Trim regularly so it does not become a sprawling, woody, unmanaged mass.

Harvest timing: Harvest stems before bloom or at early bloom. Cut back lightly to encourage branching. Do not strip the plant bare. Allow selected flowering patches for insectary function, but keep culinary sections cut.

Processing: Oregano dries very well. Hang small bundles or lay stems on screens in shade with airflow. Once fully crisp, strip leaves and store whole in airtight jars. Dried oregano is often more useful than frozen oregano.

Storage: Fresh oregano stores cold and humid for short periods. Dried oregano stores best as whole leaves in a dark, cool cabinet.

North Texas failure modes: wet winter roots; overhead irrigation; crowding; deep mulch against crown; confusing ornamental oregano with culinary oregano.

Thyme

Best North Texas role: Thyme is a low woody perennial for containers, rock edges, raised dry strips, and small harvests.

Growing preferences: Thyme needs excellent drainage, sun, airflow, and modest water. It dislikes wet clay and crown burial. Plant high, not low. Avoid heavy compost and frequent irrigation. Thyme does not belong in the same irrigation zone as mint or basil.

Planting / propagation notes: Start from transplants, plugs, or divisions. Creeping thyme and culinary upright thyme have different growth habits. Keep labels until the plant is established and the kitchen use is known. Use containers if the bed is wet or irrigation cannot be separated.

Harvest timing: Cut small sprigs as needed. For drying, harvest before or at early bloom. Avoid cutting into old woody stems without green growth below.

Processing: Thyme dries very well. Dry on screens or in small bundles. Strip leaves after drying. Store whole leaves or small stem pieces sealed and dark.

Storage: Fresh thyme stores refrigerated, wrapped loosely. Dried thyme stores well for months when kept airtight and away from heat.

North Texas failure modes: root/crown rot in wet clay; overmulching; overwatering; cutting too hard during heat; losing plants in containers that are too small and dry out daily.

Rosemary

Best North Texas role: Rosemary is a perennial evergreen culinary shrub for dry herb strips, containers, and permanent edges.

Growing preferences: Rosemary wants full sun, excellent drainage, modest fertility, and air movement. In Blackland clay, plant high in a raised bed or container. It can handle heat once established but fails in saturated soil or after severe freezes. Rosemary does not need constant water because it is evergreen. Overwatering is one of the fastest ways to kill it.

Planting / propagation notes: Transplants are standard. Start cuttings only if the grower can maintain clean, well-drained propagation conditions. Upright cultivars are better for repeated harvest; prostrate types are more ornamental and groundcover-like. Containers are useful where winter protection is needed.

Harvest timing: Harvest sprigs lightly throughout the growing season. Do not remove more than one-third of the plant at once. Avoid hard pruning into old wood after heat or freeze stress. Use regular light cuts to maintain shape.

Processing: Use fresh, dry, or freeze. Rosemary dries well on stems. Strip leaves after drying and store whole. Rosemary can also be frozen on stems or chopped.

Storage: Fresh rosemary stores cold and humid for short-term use. Dried rosemary stores sealed and dark. Use whole dried leaves and crush before cooking.

North Texas failure modes: wet feet; freeze injury; heavy pruning after stress; planting in low clay pockets; overfertility; deep mulch against the crown.

Sage

Best North Texas role: Sage is a dry herb for the Mediterranean strip, containers, and culinary harvest.

Growing preferences: Sage wants sun, drainage, and restrained water. It tolerates heat better with airflow and good root conditions. It can decline in humid, wet, or overfertilized beds. Sage does not want the same conditions as parsley or mint.

Planting / propagation notes: Transplants are easiest. Use a raised bed or container where clay drainage is questionable. Replace old woody plants if the center opens and leaf quality declines.

Harvest timing: Harvest leaves or small stems before bloom for leaf quality. Cut lightly and regularly. Avoid a hard cut in peak heat.

Processing: Sage dries well. Lay leaves or stems on screens in shade with airflow. Store dried leaves whole. Sage can also be frozen, but drying is usually more practical.

Storage: Fresh sage stores refrigerated, wrapped dry or slightly humid. Dried sage stores sealed and dark.

North Texas failure modes: wet roots; humid dense growth; old woody plants; overwatering and overfeeding; spider mites in hot dry containers.

Lavender

Best North Texas role: Lavender is a drainage-sensitive perennial trial crop, best in containers, berms, gravelly raised beds, or sharply drained edges.

Growing preferences: Lavender wants full sun, excellent drainage, low to moderate fertility, and airflow. It dislikes wet clay, rich compost pockets, humidity around the crown, and overhead irrigation. In North Texas, plant high and avoid saturated winter conditions. Lavender does not become easy because it is drought tolerant. Drought tolerance after establishment is not the same as tolerance of wet clay.

Planting / propagation notes: Choose cultivars for heat/humidity tolerance and culinary or ornamental purpose. Keep culinary and ornamental lavender separate if identity is uncertain. Use containers or berms for first trials rather than committing a low clay bed.

Harvest timing: Harvest flower stems when buds show color but before full decline. Cut stems long enough for bundles, but leave green growth below cuts.

Processing: Bundle loosely and hang upside down in shade with airflow. Once dry, strip buds for culinary or craft use only if the cultivar is appropriate. Do not make medicinal claims or essential-oil treatment claims in this chapter.

Storage: Dried lavender stores in airtight containers away from light. Fresh lavender is short-lived and should be processed quickly.

North Texas failure modes: wet clay; humidity and crown rot; overfertilizing; wrong cultivar; poor airflow; summer transplant stress.

Lemongrass

Best North Texas role: Lemongrass is a summer structural herb grown for stalks and leaves. It is heat-loving but frost-tender.

Growing preferences: Utah State Extension describes lemongrass as requiring warm, humid conditions, full sun, and plenty of moisture; it is a tender perennial commonly grown as an annual in cooler areas. In North Texas, lemongrass likes full sun, heat, steady moisture, and more fertility than rosemary or thyme. It works in large containers or bed edges. Plant after frost once soil is warm. Give space; clumps become large. Lemongrass does not belong in a dry Mediterranean herb strip. It is a warm, moist, grass-like crop.

Planting / propagation notes: Use starter plants or divisions. Grocery stalk rooting is possible but variable and should be treated as experimental unless the source is fresh and viable. Large containers make overwintering easier. In-ground plants may return after mild winters but should not be depended on without protection.

Harvest timing: Harvest outer stalks when they are thick enough to use. Cut or pull stalks from the base without destroying the entire clump. Leaves can be cut for culinary infusion.

Processing: Trim roots and tough tops from stalks. Use fresh, freeze whole stalk bases, or chop and freeze. Leaves can be dried for culinary infusion, but should be dried thoroughly to avoid mold.

Storage: Fresh stalks store refrigerated for short periods. Freeze stalks for long storage. Dried leaves store in airtight jars.

North Texas failure modes: frost; small containers drying out; underwatering in summer; crowding in small beds; assuming drought tolerance because the plant is grassy.

Bay Laurel

Best North Texas role: Bay laurel is best as a container herb so it can be protected from hard freezes and kept in well-drained media.

Growing preferences: Grow true bay laurel, Laurus nobilis, in a well-drained container with sun to light shade. Water deeply, then allow partial drying. Do not keep roots saturated. Move or protect during severe cold. Illinois Extension notes bay laurel is an evergreen shrub or tree in Zone 8 and above and is best grown in a container and moved indoors before frost in colder zones.

Planting / propagation notes: Buy a correctly labeled plant. Do not harvest from unidentified “laurel,” “cherry laurel,” “mountain laurel,” or landscape shrubs. This is a plant-identification and food-safety boundary. True bay laurel is Laurus nobilis; lookalike ornamental laurels are not interchangeable culinary plants.

Harvest timing: Harvest mature leaves. Younger leaves are more tender but mature leaves dry better. Do not strip the plant heavily.

Processing: Use fresh or dry. To dry, lay leaves flat in shade with airflow or use a low-temperature dehydrator. Store whole.

Storage: Dried bay leaves store well in airtight containers away from light. Replace when aroma fades. Use leaves to flavor food and remove before serving; do not treat the leaf as an edible green.

North Texas failure modes: wrong plant identity; wet containers; severe freeze; rootbound pot with no water in summer; overwatering in winter.

Mexican Mint Marigold

Best North Texas role: Mexican mint marigold, Tagetes lucida, often called Texas tarragon, is a heat-adapted tarragon substitute for Texas gardens.

Growing preferences: Grow in full sun to light afternoon shade with moderate water and good drainage. Texas A&M AgriLife designated Mexican mint marigold as a Texas Superstar and describes it as a hardy, easy-to-grow herb and ornamental perennial. It tolerates heat better than French tarragon and fits North Texas better than true French tarragon in many gardens. It may die back with frost and return from roots in protected sites.

Planting / propagation notes: Plant transplants after frost. Use it in the herb edge, container, or perennial pollinator strip. It can become woody if unmanaged; cut back to renew. Keep identity clear because it is a substitute for tarragon, not true French tarragon.

Harvest timing: Harvest leaves and tender stems through the warm season. Flowers can be harvested for garnish where food use is intended and identity is certain, or left for fall insectary bloom.

Processing: Use fresh or dry. Dry leaves gently; high heat reduces aroma. Store whole dried leaves in sealed jars.

Storage: Fresh stems store refrigerated for short periods. Dried leaves store dark and sealed.

North Texas failure modes: assuming it is French tarragon; wet winter crowns; frost dieback; letting old woody stems dominate; not cutting back after bloom.

Epazote

Best North Texas role: Epazote is a heat-season culinary herb used in Mexican and Central American cooking. It can self-seed and should be managed deliberately.

Growing preferences: Grow after frost in warm soil. It tolerates heat and ordinary garden soil if drainage is adequate. Use containers or an edge bed if reseeding is a concern. Epazote does not belong in medicinal-dose discussions in this crop chapter. It is treated here as a culinary herb only.

Planting / propagation notes: Direct seed or transplant after warm weather arrives. Thin plants so they do not crowd the herb edge. Remove seed heads if reseeding is not desired.

Harvest timing: Harvest young leaves and tender tips. Older stems become coarse. Use leaves before seed heads dominate.

Processing: Epazote is best used fresh. It can be dried, but aroma changes. Dry with shade airflow and store sealed. Freeze small portions if kitchen use justifies it.

Storage: Fresh epazote stores refrigerated for a short period, wrapped or in a container. For longer storage, dry or freeze small portions.

North Texas failure modes: unmanaged reseeding; coarse late-season stems; unsupported medicinal claims; treating it as a universal pest-repellent herb.

Lemon Balm

Best North Texas role: Lemon balm is a perennial mint-family herb for culinary leaves, container/edge plantings, and insectary bloom.

Growing preferences: Lemon balm prefers consistent moisture and full sun to partial shade. Utah State Extension describes lemon balm as a perennial herb in the mint family and recommends regular water with sparse fertilization; regular harvest helps contain growth. In North Texas, afternoon shade can improve leaf quality. It spreads and reseeds, though usually less aggressively than mint.

Planting / propagation notes: Use containers, edges, or dedicated perennial spots. Do not plant uncontained in a tightly managed annual crop bed. Cut back after flowering to refresh growth and limit seed. Divide or replant if the center declines.

Harvest timing: Cut stems before flowering for best leaf quality. Cut back after flowering to refresh growth and limit seed.

Processing: Use fresh, dry, or freeze. Dry on screens in shade; leaves are thin and dry quickly. Store whole dried leaves.

Storage: Fresh lemon balm stores cold and humid for short periods. Dried lemon balm loses aroma faster than rosemary or thyme, so use within the season.

North Texas failure modes: reseeding; coarse late foliage; spider mites in dry heat; weak aroma after prolonged stress; spreading into vegetable beds.

Shiso / Perilla

Best North Texas role: Shiso is a warm-season leaf herb for the moist kitchen bed or containers, with postharvest handling similar to basil because it is chilling-sensitive.

Growing preferences: Shiso likes warm soil, steady moisture, and full sun to light afternoon shade. It can self-seed aggressively in some gardens, so treat it as an intentional annual. Do not put it in a dry Mediterranean strip. It belongs with basil and other warm-season leaf herbs.

Planting / propagation notes: Start from transplants or seed after soil warms. Germination can be uneven. Keep seed lots labeled because green and red types differ in kitchen use. Manage reseeding by cutting before mature seed if unwanted.

Harvest timing: Harvest tender leaves and young shoots before flowering for best quality. Allow selected plants to flower only if seed is desired.

Processing: Use fresh or pickle/dry only with recipe-specific guidance. Freeze only if the kitchen use supports texture changes.

Storage: Store above 50°F like basil. UC Davis identifies basil and shiso as the only chilling-sensitive fresh culinary herbs in its fresh herb postharvest sheet.

North Texas failure modes: self-seeding, cold storage injury, spider mites in hot dry conditions, and quality loss after flowering.

Vietnamese Coriander

Best North Texas role: Vietnamese coriander is a heat-season leafy substitute for cilantro-like culinary uses when cilantro bolts.

Growing preferences: It likes heat, moisture, and partial shade to sun depending on water availability. It can grow in containers, moist beds, and summer herb edges. It is not the same plant as cilantro and does not produce coriander seed.

Planting / propagation notes: Propagate by cuttings or transplants. Keep soil consistently moist. In North Texas, use it as a summer bridge crop while true cilantro is out of season.

Harvest timing: Harvest tender tips and leaves regularly to keep compact growth. Do not let plants become lanky and woody.

Processing: Use fresh. Freezing or drying is secondary and changes quality.

Storage: Store cold and humid for short periods, but use quickly for best flavor.

North Texas failure modes: drought stress, full reflected heat without enough water, frost, and confusing it with true cilantro in crop records.

5. Processing Methods by Herb Type

Fresh bouquet storage

Best for basil at room temperature; cilantro, parsley, and mint in the refrigerator; lemon balm short-term; and tender herb stems that are still clean and turgid.

Method: trim stem ends; place stems in a jar with ½–1 inch of water; cover loosely with a bag; change water if cloudy; keep basil and shiso above 50°F; refrigerate cilantro, parsley, and mint.

Damp towel storage

Best for dill, parsley, cilantro, chives, mint, oregano, thyme, rosemary, and sage.

Method: sort and remove damaged leaves; if washing, dry thoroughly; wrap in a barely damp towel; place in a loose bag or container; refrigerate. Do not store herbs dripping wet. Moisture supports decay and collapses delicate leaves.

Air drying

Best for rosemary, thyme, oregano, sage, bay, lavender, mint, lemon balm, marjoram, savory, dill seed, coriander seed, and fennel seed.

Method: harvest clean stems in the morning after dew dries; bundle small amounts or spread on screens; dry in shade with airflow; avoid direct sun; strip leaves only after fully dry; store whole leaves or seeds in airtight jars.

Freezing

Best for basil, parsley, cilantro, dill, chives, lemongrass, mint, and some tender herbs where cooked use is planned.

Method: wash if needed and dry well; chop or leave whole depending on use; freeze flat in bags or in ice cube trays. Use water for soups/sauces; use oil only with extension-tested food-safety handling. Label with date.

Seed processing

Best for coriander, dill seed, fennel seed, anise/caraway where grown, and any herb grown intentionally for dry seed.

Method: let seed heads mature and brown; cut into paper bags; dry indoors in shade; rub or shake seed heads; winnow debris; store fully dry seeds in labeled jars. Texas A&M herb guidance supports drying seed heads and storing cleaned seed in labeled jars in a cool, dry, dark location.

Stalk harvest

Best for lemongrass.

Method: harvest outer stalks at the base; trim root ends and tough leaves; store fresh stalks refrigerated for short-term use or freeze whole/chopped for long storage. Dry leaves separately if they will be used in culinary infusions.

  3. Cilantro-to-Coriander Lifecycle — Cool-season leaf harvest, bolting, flowering, seed drying. Purpose: show leaf versus seed harvest timing.   4. Fennel Type Comparison — Common fennel, bronze fennel, and Florence fennel. Purpose: prevent planting the wrong type. Caption: “Common fennel does not make the Florence fennel bulb.” Alt text: “Three fennel plants side by side showing leafy common fennel, bronze fennel, and bulb-forming Florence fennel.”

  5. Drying Woody Herbs — Bundles/screens for rosemary, thyme, oregano, sage, and bay. Purpose: teach drying without mold. Caption: “Dry woody herbs in small batches, shade, and airflow; store whole leaves.” Alt text: “Herb stems drying on screens and hanging in small bundles.”

  6. Mint Containment Diagram — Mint in above-ground container versus rhizomes escaping a bed. Purpose: show containment failure. Caption: “Mint belongs in containers or controlled edges, not open annual beds.” Alt text: “Mint roots spreading beyond a bed contrasted with mint confined in a large pot.”

  7. Lemongrass Harvest and Overwintering — Outer stalk removal, container lift, frost protection. Purpose: show warm-season/frost-tender management. Caption: “Lemongrass is a warm, moist, frost-tender herb; harvest outer stalks and plan winter protection.” Alt text: “Large lemongrass clump with an outer stalk being cut and a pot moved indoors.”

  8. Herb Storage Decision Tree — Basil/shiso above 50°F, cold-humid herbs, dry woody herbs, seed storage, frozen cubes. Purpose: chapter summary visual. Caption: “Storage method follows herb type, not recipe category.” Alt text: “Decision tree for storing basil, fresh refrigerated herbs, woody dried herbs, seed herbs, and frozen herb cubes.”

8. Image-Creation Prompt for Herb Chapter

Create a cohesive educational infographic set for “Herbs in the North Texas Living Soil Garden.” Style should match the Living Soil Encyclopedia visual language: clean cream background, dark green section headers, soil cross-sections where relevant, labeled botanical illustrations, practical callouts, and no unverified numeric rates unless supplied. Do not imply images are source evidence. Include: (1) three-zone herb bed map showing dry Mediterranean strip, moist kitchen herb bed, and containment/insectary edge; (2) basil/shiso storage warning with “store above 50°F”; (3) cilantro/coriander lifecycle with leaf harvest before bolting and seed drying after maturity; (4) fennel type comparison showing common fennel, bronze fennel, and Florence fennel; (5) woody herb drying setup with rosemary, thyme, oregano, sage, bay, and lavender; (6) mint containment diagram showing container culture versus rhizome escape; (7) lemongrass harvest and frost plan; (8) herb storage decision tree. Captions must state that insectary herbs support habitat/nectar/pollen/natural enemies but do not replace scouting or guarantee pest control. Avoid medicinal claims, essential-oil treatment claims, companion-planting miracle claims, or unsupported pest-repellent claims.

Tissue Testing

What is tissue testing?

Tissue testing measures mineral nutrients in dried plant tissue. It is a historical nutrient record: what the plant has accumulated up to the sampling date. It is not the same as sap analysis, which measures dissolved nutrients in fresh plant sap and is often used for current-status interpretation.

Tissue testing is useful when visual symptoms are unclear, when a fertility program needs calibration, or when soil-test sufficiency does not match plant performance. It can help separate true deficiency from lockout, antagonism, salinity, root stress, or misread symptoms.

When is it the right tool?

Use tissue testing when a crop is repeatedly underperforming, when micronutrient deficiency is suspected, when leaves show a consistent pattern across a block, or when you need to verify whether an amendment program is moving plant nutrition in the intended direction. It is especially useful when paired with a soil test taken from the same area.

Do not use it as a panic button after the crop is already collapsing. By the time a dried-tissue report comes back, the result may explain what happened more than it rescues the current plant.

How should I sample?

Follow the lab's crop-specific instructions. The sampled leaf position matters. Some labs request the most recently mature leaf; others request a defined leaf at a defined growth stage. Do not mix old leaves, new leaves, diseased tissue, dust-covered leaves, and random plant parts in the same bag unless the lab protocol calls for it.

Collect from multiple representative plants in the same management zone. Avoid edge plants unless the edge is the problem being tested. Do not sample immediately after foliar sprays, heavy rain, drought shock, pesticide application, or unusual stress unless the lab specifically wants that condition captured. Keep samples labeled by crop, cultivar, bed, date, growth stage, and symptom status.

How do I interpret results?

A tissue report is not a recipe by itself. Compare the result with soil pH, EC, sodium, moisture, root health, crop stage, and symptoms. Low iron or manganese in tissue on high-pH soil may reflect availability or uptake limitation, not absence in the soil. Low calcium in fruiting crops may reflect water transport and root stress. High potassium can antagonize magnesium or calcium. High nitrogen can worsen lush growth and disease susceptibility.

Use lab recommendations, product labels, and extension or qualified agronomic guidance before applying trace minerals. Boron, copper, zinc, manganese, molybdenum, and other trace elements have narrow safety margins.

North Texas note

In alkaline North Texas clay, tissue testing is most useful when soil reserves look adequate but plants still show chlorosis, stunting, poor fruiting, or weak new growth. High pH, bicarbonates, salinity, sodium, compaction, and water swings can block uptake even when the nutrient exists in the soil. Pair tissue results with a soil or media test and water-quality context before adding more mineral to the bed.

Soil Microscopy Equipment

What is the tool for?

Soil microscopy is a practitioner monitoring tool for observing major soil-biological groups: fungal hyphae, protozoa, nematodes, organic fragments, and general bacterial presence. It is not a stand-alone soil-health score, pathogen diagnosis, or proof that a bed has the “right” biology.

The microscope helps answer practical questions: are fungal hyphae visible? Are protozoa active? Are nematodes present? Does the compost look aerobic and biologically active? Are observations improving over time under the same method?

What microscope is enough?

An entry-level compound microscope with 40x, 100x, and 400x objectives, LED illumination, and a mechanical stage can show nematodes, larger fungal structures, and protozoa. At 400x, bacterial morphology is illustrative only; you cannot identify bacterial species, function, or soil health from cocci and rods in a casual field slide.

A stronger home setup adds phase contrast. Phase contrast improves visibility of transparent organisms and hyphae without staining and is common in Soil Food Web-style practitioner work. It still does not turn visual morphology into definitive microbial identification.

A professional setup may include a trinocular head, camera mount, better plan-achromatic objectives, dark-field or shadow-casting capability, and calibrated counting tools. This helps with documentation and repeatability, not with bypassing training.

What supporting gear matters?

Use clean slides, cover slips, disposable pipettes, small sample jars or bags, distilled or filtered water, a timer, a measuring spoon or syringe, labels, and a worksheet. A camera is useful for consistency and later review. Keep a dedicated cleaning routine so old biofilm, dust, fibers, or dried samples do not become false observations.

What are the limits?

Microscopy can support trend monitoring, compost QA, and field learning. It cannot reliably diagnose Pythium, Phytophthora, Fusarium, root-knot nematode species, bacterial pathogens, or viral disease from casual morphology. Suspected plant disease still needs symptoms, context, extension support, or lab diagnosis.

Fungal-to-bacterial ratios derived from microscopy are method-specific practitioner estimates. Observer training, dilution, field-of-view selection, cover-slip pressure, clay background, and counting rules all affect results. Use the same method each time if you want trends.

North Texas note

Houston Black clay and other fine-textured North Texas soils can make slides visually noisy. Clay plates, organic fragments, root hairs, and fibers can be mistaken for biological targets. Good equipment helps, but sample prep and consistent dilution matter more. For clay-heavy samples, compare the standard dilution with a more dilute slide and record exactly what was used.

LSE-220 — Living Mulch Under Main Crops

Living mulch is a managed plant cover grown beneath or between a primary crop while that crop is still in production. It may be clover under widely spaced tomatoes, alyssum along a bed edge, cowpeas between young okra, thyme in a perennial herb border, buckwheat in a shoulder-season gap, or a carefully managed volunteer cover in a no-till system. Unlike straw, leaves, compost, or wood chips, living mulch is alive. That is both its value and its risk.

A dead mulch protects the surface without taking water or nutrients through roots. A living mulch protects the surface while also photosynthesizing, transpiring, rooting, flowering, competing, hosting insects, and changing the microclimate. It feeds soil biology with living root exudates, holds soil against erosion, reduces raindrop impact, shades the surface, suppresses some weed germination, and may provide nectar and pollen for beneficial insects. At the same time, it can steal water, tie up nutrients, shade seedlings, increase humidity, interfere with harvest, harbor pests, and become a weed if it sets seed or spreads beyond control.

The locked position is simple: living mulch is not free mulch. It is a controlled-competition tool. It works when the main crop remains dominant, irrigation is adequate, fertility is managed, and the living cover is kept low, sparse, or spatially separated. It fails when the cover becomes a second crop fighting the crop you meant to grow.

Species choice determines most of the outcome. White clover behaves differently from annual ryegrass, alyssum, cowpea, vetch, creeping thyme, buckwheat, or a low herb mixture. Important traits include height, rooting depth, water demand, nitrogen demand, shade tolerance, mowing tolerance, flowering period, reseeding tendency, invasiveness risk, compatibility with foot traffic, host status for pests or disease, and ease of termination. A legume may fix nitrogen only if the correct rhizobia are present and nodulation succeeds. Even then, most of that nitrogen stays inside the living plant until tissue is cut, roots turn over, or residue decomposes. Living clover does not automatically feed a tomato beside it in real time.

The best fits are systems where the main crop is large enough to dominate the cover. Wide-spaced transplanted crops such as tomatoes, peppers, eggplant, okra, trellised cucumbers, and corn edges can sometimes tolerate a living mulch if it is low and managed. Perennial systems such as orchard rows, berry alleys, herb borders, and pathway edges are often better candidates because mowing, clipping, or spatial separation can keep competition predictable. Beneficial-insect strips near beds may deliver much of the ecological benefit without putting living roots directly under shallow vegetable crops.

Poor fits are usually shallow-rooted, direct-seeded, slow-starting, or tightly spaced crops. Carrots, beets, radishes, turnips, onions, baby greens, lettuce, cilantro, dill, and small seedlings can be suppressed quickly by a cover that seems harmless beside a larger transplant. Containers and narrow beds also have less buffer. In drought-stressed systems, living mulch can turn a water problem into crop failure.

Implementation should start with dominance. Establish the main crop first unless the design is intentionally built around an overwintered cover. Keep a bare or dead-mulch ring around transplants so the stem and root crown are not crowded. Use drip irrigation to serve the main crop, not broadcast irrigation that encourages the cover to become lush. Clip, shear, mow, or crimp living mulch before it shades, climbs, seeds, lodges, or competes strongly. Terminate early if the cash crop slows down. The point is not to prove that living roots are always good; the point is to keep the right living roots in the right place at the right intensity.

Living mulch interacts with irrigation more than many gardeners expect. Because it transpires, it increases total water demand. A bed with clover or cowpea cover may need more water than the same bed under straw. In a mild spring this may be manageable. In July heat, it may be the difference between a resilient bed and a stressed one. Drip layout must prioritize the primary crop. If emitters only wet the living mulch or if the living mulch intercepts surface moisture, the main crop can decline while the bed still looks green.

It also interacts with nitrogen. Legume living mulches can support nitrogen inputs over time, but living tissue is not the same as released fertility. Cutting can return some nitrogen to the surface residue pool, but decomposition timing depends on moisture, temperature, residue size, and microbial activity. Grass living mulches can immobilize nitrogen when clipped residue decomposes. For heavy feeders, living mulch does not remove the need for soil testing, compost quality, crop-stage fertility, or observation.

Pest and disease effects are mixed. Flowering covers can feed hoverflies, parasitoid wasps, predatory insects, and pollinators when bloom timing aligns. Dense undergrowth can also shelter slugs, pillbugs, rodents, ants, cutworms, stink bugs, leafhoppers, spider mites, or flea beetles depending on the species and season. Under tomatoes, cucurbits, and peppers, thick living cover may increase humidity near the lower canopy and reduce airflow. In humid spring conditions, that can increase disease pressure. Living mulch is an IPM layer, not a substitute for scouting.

Checking results requires looking at the main crop, not admiring the cover. Monitor leaf color, internode length, flowering, fruit set, root competition, and water stress. Compare with a similar bed under dead mulch. If the living-mulch bed is smaller, paler, slower, or more drought-stressed, reduce or terminate the cover. Inspect at soil level for slugs, pillbugs, rodents, ants, cutworms, and damp disease conditions. Check whether the cover is setting seed. For legumes, inspect nodules if nitrogen fixation is part of the justification.

Living mulch has a lifecycle. It may be seeded before the crop, after transplanting, after canopy establishment, during a shoulder season, or in a pathway rather than the bed. It may need mowing, clipping, tarping, frost termination, crimping, smothering, or hand removal. Perennial living mulches can become difficult to remove once established. Annual covers must be terminated before seed set unless reseeding is intended. Seed quality matters: species, cultivar, germination rate, weed seed content, hard seed percentage, inoculation requirements, and seed treatment status should all be checked.

North Texas growing note: In Blackland Prairie clay and north DFW heat, living mulch should be used conservatively under annual vegetables. In spring it may look like perfect soil armor; by July it can become a water competitor. Living mulch is often safer in paths, bed shoulders, orchard alleys, berry rows, and cool-season windows than directly under shallow-rooted vegetables. Where summer irrigation is abundant and drip zones are well designed, low covers can be trialed under robust crops like okra, trellised tomatoes, or peppers, but straw, shredded leaves, or composted mulch are usually easier and safer for high-rotation annual beds.

Related: manuscript anchors: Cover Crop Selection; Mulch Types and Selection; Successional Ecology in Soils; Crop Rotation by Family; Hybrid Irrigation Design; Beneficial Predators and Habitat; Weed Management; No-Till Bed Establishment.

    # Editorial Batch 05 / Master Batch 3B

    LSE-221 — Companion Planting (Mechanistic Frame)

    Companion planting is the intentional placement of two or more plant species near one another to influence pest pressure, pollination, space use, microclimate, nutrient cycling, weed suppression, or beneficial-insect activity. The older version of the idea often appears as charts of “good companions” and “bad companions.” The manuscript position is different. Companion planting is useful only when the pairing has a plausible mechanism and fits the crop, pest, timing, spacing, water, and region.

    A mechanistic companion system is not plant friendship. It is design. Trap crops concentrate a pest on a plant that is more attractive than the cash crop. Insectary plants provide nectar, pollen, shelter, or alternate prey for predators and parasitoids. Repellent or masking plants may interfere with host-finding cues, visual contrast, or landing behavior in specific pest-crop situations. Physical companions provide shade, wind buffering, trellis support, ground cover, or canopy layering. Nutrient companions such as legumes are used in rotations, cover crops, or biomass cycles to influence fertility over time. Weed-suppression companions use living mulch or dense cover to reduce bare soil. Temporal companions fill space briefly before a slower crop closes canopy.
